151. JAMES TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of Oct., 250 yards of canvass, value 14 l.; 69 yards of baize, 4 l. 15 s.; 60 yards of trimming, 4 l.; 60 yards of binding, 15 s.; 1 copper boiler, 12 s.; 360 lamps, 2 l. 5 s.; 72 drinking-glasses, 2 l.; 3 decanters, 10 s.; 60 mugs, Il.; 6 metal taps, 125.; 36 knives, 8 s.; 36 forks, 7 s.; 8 pewter measures, 6 s.; 11 dishes, 10 s.; 60 plates, 6 s.; 3 sets of cruets, 5 s.; 3 stands for cruets, 2 s. 6 d.; 5 saucers, 7 d.; 1 cup, 1 d.; 3 tea-pots, 1 s. 6 d.; 3 jugs, 6 d.; 3 keys, 9 s.; 3 tin boilers, 6 s.; 1 baking-tin, 2 s.; 6 salt-cellars, 3 s.; 3 punch-bowls, 7 s.; 8 flags, 8 s.; and 1 tea-urn, 9 s.; the goods of George William Henry Young: and JOHN RAYNER , for feloniously receiving 14 driuking-glasses, 9 cruets, 1 salt-cellar, 5 saucers, 1 cup, 3 pewter-measures, measures, 2 dishes, 14 plates, 26 jugs, 9 yards of baize, 20 yards of canvass, and 2 flags, part of the said goods, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.; to which
TAYLOR pleaded GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Six Months .
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE WILLIAM HENRY YOUNG . I am a hawker and pedlar. I live at the Old King Harry, in the Mile-end-road—I generally travel the country with a van and Sheffield cutlery—I sleep in the van when I am traveling On the 9th of Oct. I was at Croydon in the morning, and at Mile-end in the evening—I slept in the Mile-end-road all night—on that day I placed the whole of a drinking-booth in a loft belonging to Mr. Walton, who keeps the Old King Harry—it consisted of the canvass and other things stated—I valued them at about 30 l.—on the 30th of Oct. I received information, and came to town—I went to Mr. Walton's, and found my goods were gone—I know the prisoner Taylor—I had given him an odd job now and then, but not regularly—I gave a description of him, I sod after some hours'search I found him near Ratcliff-highway—he took me to Mr. Wren's, in Rosemary-lane—I saw Rayner there on the top of the stairs—he was in his night-gown, just got out of bed—he came down stairs to me—I said to Taylor, "Is this the man you sold the goods to?" he said, Yes, it was—Rayner said it was a bad job, he could not get them back that night, he might the next day, or he could the next day, or some such words—a friend called in some policemen—they searched the house, and some goods were found, some bits of green-baize, some crockery, some glass, a piece of canvass, and a couple of small flags—they were shown to me, and I knew most of them as my own property—it was rather late in the evening—I should say it was after eleven—I saw no pewter measures then—I have seen some since, and a copper boiler, a gravy-dish, and other things—I believe them to be mine—most of them I can swear to—they were some of the articles I had left in the loft.
BENJAMIN BOORN . I am a hawker, and am the prosecutor's brother-in-law, law, I went with him to Rayner's on the 30th of Oct;—I saw the articles found, which the prosecutor has mentioned—when I went in Rayner was in bed—I went up stairs and called him—he opened his room door, and came on the landing in his shirt—he then dressed himself, and came down—Young said, "I am come about my things"—Rayner said, "I can't get them back to-night, nor yet to-morrow,"but Taylor had before that stamped his foot on the ground, and said, "The things are here"—while Rayner was in the shop, a man, dressed as a sailor, came in and said, "What is the matter?"—I said, "Mind your own business"—Rayner nodded his head to the sailor, as much as to say, "Go out"—the sailor went into the back room, got a light, and went up stairs—some policemen were called in—I went up stairs with them to Rayner's bed-room—we found in his bed-room this piece of baize, and the narrow piece of canvass which bad been cut off the baize; and on the top of the drawers in the room we found some glasses and some cruets—I could swear to two of the dram-glasses, and to the piece of baize—I went into the adjoining room, and found some dishes and plates, which I believe to be my brother-in-law's property—they had been through my hands several times—I went up stairs, and saw the sailor in the front room at the top of the house—he had a light, and there were some beds which he tumbled out of a cupboard—I did not know any property found in that room—I came down, and in about half-an-hour I went up again—I saw the sliding-window in the room in which I had seen the sailor, was a little open—I shoved it right back, and got on the leads—I went as far as the next window, turning towards the left, and in the gutter I found a piece of new
canvass and two flags—I had had them in my hand before, and I believe them to be the prosecutor's—I could not swear to them—they are Union-Jack flags, and are used as handkerchiefs—they have nail-holes in them, and I have not the least doubt that they are my brother-in-law's property—this canvass is about the quantity that was left when there was a new thirty-feet tilt made—there is no mark on it—I believe it to be my brother's property.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You desired the sailor to go away, did you? A. No, I just told him to mind his own business—he was not one of the prisoners.
JULIA SHEEN . I am a widow, and live in Blue Anchor-yard, Rosemary-lane. I know the prisoner Rayner—on the 25th of Oct. I went to his shop to pay him a little money—I paid him 2 s. off a bed and bedstead, which I had bought—I came away, and went to a grocer's shop and bought some sugar—Taylor then came to me, and wanted me to buy something—I went back to Rayner's shop with Taylor, and Taylor and I, and a sailor in a flannel shirt went up stairs—Rayner did not go up—Taylor asked me a price for some crockery, which I saw upstairs and declined to buy—I went away and sent for my daughter, and she and I went back—I did not see Rayner then—Taylor and a sailor-like man sold my daughter some articles—Rayner keeps a barber's and a broker's shop—he buys old stores, and everything.
PIERCE DRISCOLL ( police-sergeant H 24.) On the 30th of Oct. I had information of this, and I apprehended the two prisoners—they were standing by one another in Rayner's shop—I asked them if they knew what they were charged with—they made no reply—I remained in the I house, and an officer took them to the station.
Cross-examined. Q. Rayner was admitted to bail? A. Yes.
ALLAN PIPE ( police-constable H 51,) I went with Driscoll to assist in taking the prisoners, and to search Rayner's house—I found the things which have been produced, and in a box under the bed I found these three pewter measures, smashed up as they are now—I was present when the prisoners were examined—after they had been cautioned, Taylor said, in presence of Rayner, that he did not know that he could make it any ✗; that these were the things he sold to Rayner—the things were then present—Rayner said he should decline saying anything, by the advice of his attorney.
RAYNER — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18441125-152]
152. WILLIAM SMELT and ANN VARRELL were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of Nov., 1 blanket, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 saucepan, 1 s. 6 d., 1 pillow, 6 d.; 1 looking-glass, (6 d.; and 1 1/2 yards of drugget, 6 d.; the goods of Thomas Howes.
THOMAS HOWES . I am a shoemaker, and live in Blue Anchor-alley, St. Luke's. The two prisoners came to me on the 24th of Oct.—they asked for ready-furnished lodgings—Smelt said he was a shoemaker, that Varrell was his wife, and she was a dressmaker—I let them a furnished room, and they took possession of it the same evening—on the 1st of Nov. Smelt left my house, leaving Varrell and a little girl there—on the following day I spoke to Varrell about the rent—she told me I should soon have it—on the next day she told me her husband was gone to Cambridge about some money, and, independent of what
he would bring back, she had money of her own, by which she would be able to pay me in a day or two—she said she had some houses on the Gravesend-road which belonged to her and her sisters, and she wanted to sell them—I said I thought I knew a person who would buy them, but there would be some difficulty about selling them, as she had a husband—on the following Wednesday I went into the room, and the fire-irons were gone, and a glass—Varrell was out at that time—I went in again, and missed the other articles stated—they were mine—here are two blankets here which are mine, and are part of the things I missed—when I missed the things, I asked Varrell where they were—she said they were pledged, that her husband had the duplicates with him, and he was gone into the country—she said, "We took them jointly,"and she gave me a list of the places where the things were pledged—I gave her into custody—she said she took them from distress, and she was in distress, and the Magistrate, in consequence of that, sent her to the workhouse—I saw Smelt again on the Sunday evening following, and he was then in custody.
BENJAMIN ROBERTS ( police-sergeant G 1.) Varrell was given to me by Mr. Howes. She gave me a list of where she had pawned the things, and said she took them to the pawnbroker's herself—I found them all correct, as she had given me the account—several of the things were given up by the pawnbrokers.
THOMAS FEW ( police-constable G 154.) I went to St. Luke's work-house, and took Smelt—I told him he was charged with stealing some blankets and other things—he denied taking any, and said be knew nothing about it, that he went into the country and left his wife—I took him to the station.
HENRY PERCEVAL WALKER I am assistant to Mr. Chapman, a pawnbroker. I produce these two blankets, pawned by the prisoner Varrell.
Varrell. You did not take both the blankets in of me.
Witness. I was present, and saw them both taken in of you.
COURT. Q. Can you swear she is the woman that pledged them? A. Yes, and I will swear I took one of them in of her.
Smelts Defence. I am perfectly innocent of the goods that were pledged; I know if my wife did it, it was through utter distress, and I have no doubt they were not taken with any felonious intent; no persons suffered more than we have; we have been nine nights walking about the country roads; I had been a teetotaller, but I left them, and a parcel of people have calumniated me.
> Varrell's Defence. Mr. Howes came on the Wednesday, and asked if the things were all right; I said they were not, but I would write into the country for some money; I wrote a letter, and he told me he posted it; after that I and my little girl laid down, as we had no fire, but he brought in two policemen, and would not allow me to stay in the house till I got an answer to my letter; he asked me for a copy of my mother's will; I gave it him, and he never returned it; I did some work for his wife which came to half-a-crown, and he had a shilling off the rent—I received two half-sovereigns, and they were returned back by order of the Magistrate.
NOT GUILTY ,
NEW COURT, Monday, Dec. 2 nd 1844.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18441125-153]
153. THOMAS FIELD, alias John Eaglesfield, alias William West , was indicted for obtaining money by false pretences.
MR. O'BRIEN conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM WALL . I live in Melin's-place, Westminster-road. Early in Sept. the prisoner called on me—he asked for Mr. Wall—I said I was he—he said, "No, I want an elderly person"—I said, "Then you mean my predecessor in business"—he said, "No, a Mr. Wall, who previously lived in Union-place, Lambeth"—I said it was my father, but he had been dead eight or nine years"—he said, "Has he, indeed, I have some good news for you"—I asked him in, and he said there was some property unclaimed in the Court of Chancery belonging to me—we held a conversation about my father's family and so on, and I asked him how to get the property—he said I must get an order under the Court of Chancery, the use of which was to search in Doctors' Commons and examine the will—he showed me several orders, similar to those before me—he said the expense would be 5 s. 6 d.—I made an appointment to see him the next day—he called, but I was not at home—he called a day or two afterwards—I saw him, and told him I would give him 5 s. 6 d. if he would procure this order from the Court of Chancery—he called in the evening, brought the order, and I gave him 5 s. 6 d.—I believed it was an order from the Court of Chancery—he represented it to be so, and I paid him two half-crowns and a sixpence—I took the order in my hand previous to paying him the money—this is the order ( looking at it)—I had it in my possession five or six weeks—he said he was not bound to say what the property consisted of—I asked him if it was freehold or funded—he said he thought it consisted of both, and he thought it amounted to about 1900 l.
Prisoner. Q. When did I call on you? A. Early in Sept.—you asked my name, as I have already stated—you said it was not me you wanted—you said it was my father—I told you he was dead, and you said you had some good news for me—I have no recollection of saying that the descendants of my father might make application by letters of administration—you said you came from an office connected with the Court of Chancery—you gave me a card with West and Co. on it—you said, "That is my card, if you have any communication to make, it must be by letter"—there was West, or his agent, on the card—I understood you to be a principal—you agreed to meet me next day—when you introduced this order, you said something about its being given you as an extract from the Master's office. I do not admit that you did say so—I cannot recollect—you said, after the order was procured it must be taken to Doctors' Commons, and the costs of it would be 6 s., to look after a will if there was any there—that was for your trouble, I suppose, in examining the will—you left then, and was absent four or five weeks, and called again to know if I had further considered the matter—I said I had given the order to my solicitor, and I gave you his address—I employed you about a bill of exchange—you represented you could discount me a bill.
JOHN HARDING . I am a boot-maker, and live in Mary-street, Stangate.
The prisoner came to me last July and asked me if my name was John Harding—I told him it was—he said he had come concerning property which had been laid by for sixty years and would go to the Crown—he did not say what property—he said he came as an uninterested person—I began to tell him about my family and my forefathers—he said it was right, and I ought to prefer my claim—I said I would consider of it—he came again the first week in Sept., and said I must get an order from the Court of Chancery, and that would cost me 5 s. 6 d.—he showed me a small paper, which he said was an order from the Court of Chancery, and asked me for 5 s. 6 d. for my demand—he wrote the account which I stated to him, and he said, "If they approve of this statement, the Court of Chancery will grant you an order"—this was on a Thursday morning—he came again in the evening and brought this order folded up—I said, "Did you write this?"—he said, "No, I did not write it myself, the Court of Chancery wrote this"—I believed it came from the Court of Chancery, and, believing that, I gave him half-a-crown, and told him if he would call in a day or two I would give him the balance—he said it was a considerable deal of landed property, and I understood in the county of Hampshire—I did not pay the prisoner any more money.
Prisoner. Q. Will you state to the Court the first conversation that took place? A. I do not know how that is possible, if I were to relate all, it would take from now till Christmas—I suppose the first time you were with me an hour, or an hour and a half—it is impossible for me to tell what you related, from the multiplicity of the words—you told me you did not know whether I was the person, and after conversation you said I was, and advised me by all means to get an order from the Court of Chancery—I did not go to the Commons—you said the expense would be 6 s.—5 s. for your expenses, and 1 s. for searching the will".
EDWARD HAMMOND . I live in Hamilton-row, and am a shoemaker. On the 18th of Oct. the prisoner came to my father's shop, and asked me what was my father's name—I told him, and said he was born at Gosport—he said there was 450 l. left to me somewhere in Gosport, and the money was in the Court of Chancery—he told me where he himself could be found, and gave me an order similar to this one—he said it was an order from the Court of Chancery, and he said it was necessary to write a petition—he had 5 s. 6 d. from me for this paper—he wrote a petition in my house, and took the parchment away with him—I had not a farthing in the house—Mr. Porthouse lent me the money, two half-crowns and a sixpence—I did not see the prisoner again for three weeks, and then he came with the petition, and wanted 1 s. 3 s., but he did not have that—I found there was no property at Gosport.
Prisoner. Q. Did you ever pay me any money at all? A. I did not, but the person I got to pay it did—Mr. Porthouse paid it in my presence, and I am answerable to him for it—I cannot recollect that you informed me, if I was not the right party the money would be returned—I did not see you for three weeks after, and I thought I should never see your face again—when you came again I gave you into custody.
ASEL PORTHOUSE . I recollect seeing the prisoner and Hammond in Oct. last—I paid the prisoner 5 s. 6 d. by Hammond's desire.
Prisoner. Q. Did you understand that if he was not the right party the money was to be returned? A. No—if he was not the right party they would not grant the order at the Court, and the thing would be
dropped—I said, "If this comes from the Court of Chancery there must be something in it,"and I advanced him the money to pay it.
CHARLES STODHART . I am a solicitor. I am acquainted with the affairs of the Court of Chancery, and the manners of that Court—then documents are appearance papers in Common-law Courts—when a person is served with a copy of a writ we file them in the Court of Queen's Bench Common Pleas, and Exchequer—these are not in any way connected with the Court of Chancery—they are always on paper, not on parchment, as these are—I swear that these cannot have issued from the Court of Chancery, or the Master's office.
Prisoner's Defence. I trust, in giving you a statement of the facts of the case, you will he able to give me the benefit of an acquittal. The greater part of my life has been spent in business, and having some small resources for old age, I thought I was independent; but in consequence of calamities, one of which was fire, I was reduced, in forty-eight hours, from ✗ ence to penury. I was unable to follow the profession I had been in, and was engaged as commission-agent to two gentlemen of the highest respectability. ability. Mr. West employed me to find out parties that he gave me instruction had property in the Court of Chancery. I had to walk very often from one part of London to the other to find the right parties; if I found them I had a small commission; if not, I had my labour for my pains Mr. West lives at No. 18, Throgmorton-street, and I do not consider I am the responsible person in this case. I only acted according to my instructions, and am entirely innocent of any guilty intention, in reference to the orders that are given me by my employer: through not being able to Inverse, I have not been able to get Mr. West and other witnesses for my defence; I came here not prepared for my defence, Mr. West having employed me. I made numbers of applications, and where the party has not been the right heir to the property, it has been no charge or expense to them. I do not know whether they are entitled to the property or not; that has to be proved, according to the nature of the case. I beg to state, that the answer to each of the cases that have come this morning is to be given in Nov., and without waiting for the expiration of that month, I was brought here without witness or evidence, or even the evidence of Mr. West. I am brought here like a man bound hand and foot, and am rendered totally defenceless. If I had employed a party to embody these particulars, I could prove I am not guilty of the charge. I leave my case for your consideration. I solemnly declare I had no intention to wrong any individual; and I trust you will give me the benefit of your merciful consideration.
GUILTY . Aged 65— Transported for Seven Years .
( See Humphery, Mayor; Eighth Session, page 470.)
First Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

154. EDWARD HENLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of Nov., at St. Mary, Islington, 13 spoons, value 3 l. 9 s.; 1 cream ewer, 1 l.; 1 pair of sugar-tongs, 10 s.; 1 watch, 1 l.; 1 pair of earrings, 6 d.; and 1 brooch, 1 s.; the goods of William Berry, in the dwelling-house of George Havinden: JOHN MATHAM , for feloniously receiving 1 watch, value 1 l.; 1 pair of earrings, 6 d.; part of the said goods; well knowing them to have been stolen: and THOMAS CARROLL , for feloniously receiving 1 cream ewer, value 1 l.; and 5 spoons, 1 l. 10 s.; part of the said goods; well knowing them to have been stolen.
ELIZABETH BERRY . I am the wife of William Berry—we live at No. 17, Richard-street, Islington—I am Henley's aunt, and he lived with me—I had a box containing plate—I saw it safe on Sunday morning, the 10th of Nov., and I missed it on the Monday—Henley had access to the box—I never saw the other two prisoners there—I was at home all day that Sunday—I live in the dwelling-house of George Havinden—it is in the parish of Islington.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How long had Henley been away from you? A. About a week previous to that—he had lived with me from the death of his mother—he has been an honest boy—Mr. Havinden lives in the house—it is in the parish of St. Mary, Islington.
JOHN ROWE ( police-constable B 91.) I went to a house in George-street, St. Giles's, about nine at night, on Friday, the 19th of Nov.—the landlord of the house is named Sands—I found Henley and Matham there—I took Henley to the station, I then took Matham—I did not tell him the charge against him, but he said he did not care a b—r about it—Henley said that the landlord had got the property which he had taken from his uncle (by the landlord, he meant Carroll, who is called the landlord of that house, because he has the management of it—the proper landlord does not live there)—Henley afterwards said, that Matham was with him at the time he committed the robbery.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Was not what Henley said, "The landlord has got the things that have been taken from my uncle?" A. No—he said, "that I took from my uncle"—I did not take a note of the conversation—there were several other persons in the room, perhaps eight or nine—I did not notice who they were—there was not a great deal of confusion—I went into the kitchen and fetched him out—I did not tell him it would be better for him to confess—I said, "I Want you to go to the station"—he said, "But I will have things righted before I leave the house"—I did not know at that time the charge he was wanted on—Carroll's wife had told me a boy was wanted, and he was in their house.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. Do you know anything of this house? A. I know it is a lodging-house—I have seen the landlord there—he lives within three or four doors of the house—Henley said the property had been given to the landlord.
ROBERT HICKS ( police-constable B 48.) On Friday night, the 15th of Nov., I followed Carroll to a tobacconist's shop, and saw him offer these two spoons, which have been identified by Mrs. Berry—I took him into custody, tody, and asked him where he got the spoons he was offering in the tobacconist's shop—he denied offering any spoons, and then asked me what business it was of mine—I told him if he did not give me a satisfactory answer I should take him into custody—he said, "For God's sake, policeman, don't take and lock me up, I will give you all the money I possess and all my property"—on the way to the station he pulled this cream ewer out of his pocket, and attempted to throw it away—I took it from his hand, and put it in my own pocket—I got him to the station, and found this watch and all the rest of this property on him—he then said it was his own property, and he had been to fetch it to carry home.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLOR. Q. Have you not given a very different ferent account of this before? A. Not at all—I stated before that he attempted to throw part of the plate away—Carroll was not very drunk—he was the worse for liquor—he could walk without leading very well—I
never said he was drunk—I saw him offering these spoons for sale, and when he came out I asked him where he got them—I was outside the shop, and saw him through the window—I know he was offering them for sale because I heard him—it is a very small shop—it is in a public thoroughfare—there roughfare—there were no carriages passing—there were not a great number of persons in the street—I swear he offered these for sale—he pulled the money out of his pocket, and said, "I will give you all the money I have, and all the property I have got about me, if you don't lock me up"—he did not say he had been sent to sell them for another person—I am confident I have not made a mistake—he said he had a wife about to be confined.
JOHN O'BRIEN . I keep a house, No. 7, George-street—these prisoners were found at No. 5—on the Sunday afternoon after the robbery, about half-past two, Matham and one or two men were drinking in my house—Matham asked me what o'clock it was—I told him my watch did not gohe pulled out a watch like this one produced, but there are thousands like it in London—he told me his watch was right, and I set mine by it—he had a pair of coral earrings.
MRS. BERRY. This is my property—a boy found the box at the back of the layers in the Liverpool-road.
HENLEY — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months .
CARROLL — GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined One Year .
MATHAM — NOT GUILTY .
ESSEX CASES.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

157. HENRY JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of Nov., 4 handkerchiefs, value 10 s. 6 d.; and 1 pair of shoes, 85.; the goods of Joseph Johnson.
JOSEPH JOHNSON . I live in West-street, Stratford. The prisoner is my brother, and he occasionally lived in the same house with me. On Thursday evening, the 21st of Nov., I came home, and went up stairs to put away a little money in a box—I found my box had been broken open, and missed from it three handkerchiefs—I came down, and missed a pair of shoes and another handkerchief—I went to the station, and gave information, and the prisoner was taken by a policeman—the policeman took a handkerchief from the prisoner at the station, in my presence—it was one of those which I had missed from below stairs—it was a cotton one, and I have the fellow one to it on my neck now—it is my mother's misfortune to be blind, and she is lying on her bed—any one can get into the house without being seen.
Prisoner. There is my sister-in-law there, who is not out of the house five minutes in a day.
Witness. She lives there, but she was in a room up stairs, and has her husband to attend to.
SAMUEL BENTON ( police-constable K 49.) I took the prisoner, and told him what he was charged with—he said he had not taken anything—he had a handkerchief round his neck—I took it off—he said it was his, bnt it was claimed by the prosecutor.
JOSEPH JOHNSON re-examined. This is my handkerchief—it is hemmed with white cotton, the same as the one I have round my neck—I bought the two and my little niece hemmed them for me—there is no name or mark to it—I do not know that my niece ever did anything for the prisoner—he never had anything of his own—he has done no work for four years—he has got a job now as a bricklayer's labourer, and will not do it—he sent another person to do it.
Prisoner. I own to its being his handkerchief; my being down stairs;
I merely put it round my neck while my own was washed; the other things I know nothing about.
COURT to JOSEPH JOHNSON. Q. Do you know whether he had one that wanted washing? A. I cannot say—I was not at home—I work from six o'clock till eight at night.
JURY. Q. Have you lent him handkerchiefs? A. No, I would not—I knew I could not trust anything with him—my mother would lend him anything almost.
NOT GUILTY

[trialAccount t18441125-158]
158. JOHN DRURY was indicted for feloniously uttering a forged request for the delivery of 6 quarts of beer, well knowing it to have been forged, with intent to defraud William Frederick Nash.
JOHN WATTS . I live at Royal-hill, Greenwich. On the 15th of Nov. I was in Nash's beer-shop—the prisoner came in, and said, "Mrs. Nash, I have got an order for six pots of beer, from a young man named Bush, who works opposite the Circus"—he produced a paper, but Mrs. Nash still doubted it—she had only a gallon can—she said, "This can will not hold it; you have a half-gallon can at the building, you ought to have brought that with you"—he said, "Oh, that will hold it"—she put four pots and a pint into the gallon can, and desired me to take the rest down to the building with him—when I got to the comer of the Circus, he said, "Stop; do you see that man going up the plank on the scaffolding, that is our foreman; I do not want him to see us, so you go round that way, and you will find young Bush at the further end; tell him to come round to the back of the building to me;"'and when I got to Bush, he was astonished—the prisoner took the can of beer down to his lodging, instead of going to the back of the building.
Prisoner. Q. Did not I tell you to go to Bush, and tell him I had received six pots of beer? A. Yes.
EMMA RHODA NASH . The prisoner produced a paper to me, which I produce—Bush was in the habit of sending tickets for beer, and I let the prisoner have the beer mentioned—( read —"Please, let the bearer have six pots of beer. H. BUSH.")
HENRY BUSH . I did not authorise the prisoner to get this beer in my name—I never allow the men to have beer without I give a ticket—I did not give him this ticket on the 15th of Nov.—it is not my writing, nor was it written by my authority—I did not send him with it, or know of his going.
Prisoner. Q. On Friday afternoon, at half-past two o'clock, did not you give me an order for three pots of beer? A. Yes, and you had an order for two pots in the morning—this is not one of them—I do not know whose writing this order is.
JOHN DAVIS ( policeman.) I apprehended the prisoner in a public-house—he was pointed out to me by the prosecutor—I said, "You are charged with getting six pots of beer in another man's name"—he said a man gave him the ticket, and he was answerable for the beer—he said,
"There is no name signed to the paper, and what can they make of it?"—on going to the station, he said he took the beer home to his own lodging—that was the day after it happened.
Prisoner. Q. Did not I say, "What is the charge for? I am guilty of no crime, I shall pay for the beer? A. He said he would pay for it if I allowed him to wait.
Prisoner. The prosecutor would not wait till I received the money for it on Saturday night. At half-past two o'clock, on the Friday, Bush gave me an order for three pots of beer; the rest of the men said, "Are not you going to send us a drop?" I said, "I don't know." I was intoxicated, and not capable of writing. I met a young man, called Roving Dan, who 1 was very happy to see, understanding he had been killed in the tunnel. I said to him, "Go back to Bush, and ask him to make this order into six quarts of beer." I thought he went to him, but it seems he did not; he returned to me with this paper. 1 did not know it was not Bush's writing. I went to the public-house, and told the publican I had an order for six pots, and the man came with me with the three pints. I told him to go to Bush and say I had received the six pots of beer; but he did not return, and I went home with it to my lodging to treat the young man. I was willing to pay for the beer.
HENRY BUSH re-examined. I was at work on the premises at the time three pints of the beer was brought to me by Watts—he said, "Here is three pints of beer, where shall I take it to?"—I laid, "What three pints?"—he said, a man had ordered six pots of beer, and told him to bring three pints that way, and he would bring the rest round to the back—I had given no such order—the prisoner was not at work that day—I did not have three pots and two pots—he and Roving Dan had them to themselves—they asked me to give an order for two pots at half-past eight o'clock, which I did—I did not see them again till half-past twelve, when the prisoner came round and said, "I have got my mate with me, I don't want to send him home without any beer, will you give me an order for three pots?" which I did—I was not consulted about the order for six—I wrote the orders which I gave before—the beer was to come from the same place.
MRS. NASH re-examined. I had an order for two pots about ten minutes before the prisoner came for the six—a little boy brought it—he came first without an order, and I sent him back for one—there was no order for three pots delivered, only for two pots—the prisoner had two pots at the bar with his mate, without an order—he was drinking in the bar at the time Bush passed by—my husband would not let him have more beer without an order, and he called Bush in to ask him for an order for two pots, which he gave verbally—I had no order for three pots—he got the six pots at half-past three.
HENRY BUSH re-examined. I wrote the order for the three pots at half-past twelve o'clock—the order for two I wrote at the bar—he was to pay me for the beer I gave the order for—he always paid me on Saturday night.
WILLIAM FREDERICK NASH . The first thing in the morning I served the prisoner with two pots of beer without a written order—he called Bush over to acknowledge it, and Bush wrote an order at the bar for it—I did not get any order for three pots that day—I would not have trusted the prisoner with the beer himself.
NOT GUILTY .

162. THOMAS BAULSOM was indicted for stealing 6 waistcoats, value 1 l., the goods of Joseph Sharp.
JOHN EVERDEN . I am in the service of Mr. Joseph Sharp, a pawnbroker, at Deptford. We missed about forty waistcoats in June—these six are part of them, and are Mr. Sharp's property.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When they went you cannot tell? A. It was on a Saturday in June, and we found these six on the 17th of July, at the prisoner's lodging—on the 16th of July two waistcoats were offered to us, which were our own.
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant R 15.) On the 17th of July I went to the prisoner's lodging, in Giffin-street—I found these six waistcoats
between the bed and the sacking—the prisoner made his escape, and we could not find him till lately.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner was not the only person who lodged at that house? A. No, there were other persons lodged there—a woman lived in the room with the prisoner—she has been since then sent to Maidstone—the prisoner was not there at the time of the search—I had seen him there about a week before.
ELIZABETH CURTIS . The prisoner and Margaret Ford lodged with me in June last as man and wife—I was present when these waistcoats were found in the prisoner's bed-room—when the officer came, he insisted on the prisoner's room door being opened, and I got a key and opened it—the prisoner had slept there the night before, but I did not see him after nine o'clock that morning.
Cross-examined. Q. What room did the prisoner and Ford live in ✗ your house? A. They lived in the room at the top of the house first, and then I removed my own things down—I did not remove his things at all—he came in late sometimes—Ford was taken into custody on the 17th of July, and when she was taken the prisoner went away—he was not there when the waistcoats were found—I had seen the waistcoats, and lifted them out from under the bed the day before.
COURT. Q. The prisoner came in late, did he? A. Yes—my health was bad, I removed him down to the lower part of the house, and he was satisfied.
JOSEPH GREEN ( police-sergeant R 315.) I took the prisoner at Newcross—I told him ✗ wanted him for a robbery at Mr. Sharp's—he said, "Very well, I will go with you quietly, and have what I am to have, and have done with it."
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18441125-166]
166. GEORGE STOCKEY was indicted for stealing 30 sovereigns, and 20 half-sovereigns; the monies of Harriet Arnold.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
HARRIET ARNOLD . I am a widow—my husband died in March last—I kept a shop in Dartford for nineteen years—I had 218 l. in the Bank, which I drew out four or five months ago—in Oct. last I had 106 l. left in gold, and a few shillings—on the 19th of Oct. I was going to Greenwich, to take a shop there—I went into the Castle, at Woolwich—I had my money about me—I had 40 l. in a nightcap, and I lost all that—I had the rest of my money in a blue handkerchief—I was in the tap-room, at the Castle, and got the worse for what I had there—I saw the prisoner sitting by my side—I took out the nightcap, for the purpose of looking for some silver—the prisoner could see the money—I was rather intoxicated, and I turned the money out of the nightcap into my lap—the prisoner shook my apron, and it all went on the floor—I tried to pick some of it up, but the prisoner picked it up, and put it into his pocket.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Where did you find yourself the next morning? A. I was taken to the police-office after 1 was turned into the yard—the prisoner tried to get some of the money out of my hand—it was past eleven o'clock at night—I do not recollect whether there were many other persons in the tap-room—I observed other parties besides the prisoner, but they were not sitting by the side of me—the prisoner was sitting by my side—I do not recollect the prisoner offering my sovereigns to me, and my saying, "No, keep them till morning"—he forced a glass of rum on me—I went into the tap-room while it was daylight—I sat there till it was time to go to bed.
HENRY YARRELL . I am a shoemaker, and live in Charles-street, Woolwich, I was at the Castle about eleven o'clock that night, and saw
the prosecutrix—there was a piece of work, some men drinking, and I heard some money fall on the ground in the tap-room—I turned round on hearing that, and saw the prisoner, who seemed as if he wanted to get something out of the prosecutrix'a lap, and in her making resistance the remainder of the money fell on the floor—the prisoner picked it up, and I saw him go away with the money in his hand.
Cross-examined. Q. How many more persons were in the tap-room? A. Seven or eight—I was not drinking—the prisoner was sitting close by the prosecutrix—they were very confidential together—I did not see anybody pick up any of the money but the prisoner—I had seen him once before—I had never seen the prosecutrix before—I did not pick up any of the money—it was not scattered about the floor, it fell all in a lump—I did not see the prisoner offer the prosecutrix the money, nor hear her say, "Keep it till to-morrow"—the prisoner went out, and the prosecutrix was carried out nearly immediately afterwards—there were seven or eight of us there—we saw the prosecutrix in this condition, but none of us interfered.
MR. DOANE. Q. Who carried the prosecutrix out? A. A soldier named Ward carried her into the passage—I was lodging there.
MARY ANN WARD . I am cook at the Castle—my husband is a gunner in the Artillery. I remember the night of the 19th of Oct.—I saw the prisoner sitting on the bench next the fire-place in the tap-room—Mrs. Arnold was sitting next the fire, and the prisoner was on her right hand—I saw the prisoner with a handful of money, more gold than silver—Mrs. Arnold was the worse for liquor—she was trying to get the gold and silver from the prisoner's hand, and in doing that she rather slipped off her seat, and the rest of the money fell from her lap on the floor—there was more gold than silver—I saw the prisoner pick it up.
Cross-examined. Q. What time was this? A. It was between ten and eleven—I am servant at the Castle—I saw the prosecutrix in that condition, and I saw a large sum of money—I took no notice of it—I cannot say how many persons were there—some coal-porters were there very drunk.
MARY ANN WALLHOUSE . I am the wife of Thomas Wallhouse, a pensioner, living at Woolwich. The prisoner was a tenant of mine, and paid 3 s. 6 d. a week for his room—on Wednesday, the 16th, I lent him a sixpence, as he said he was short, and on Friday, the 18th, I lent him another sixpence—he always behaved remarkably well, and paid his way—I am a laundress—if he had been disposed to be dishonest he could have robbed me.
JAMES STOCKEY . I am the prisoner's brother. On the Tuesday after Saturday, the 19th of Oct., he gave me sixteen sovereigns and two half-sovereigns to take care of for him—he is a blacksmith in the Arsenal, and his wages are 24 s. 6 d. a week.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe he has been in the habit of sometimes depositing money with you? A. Yes—the largest sum he ever deposited with me at one time before this was 6 l.
WILLIAM JAMES . I am a coalporter, and live at Woolwich. On the night of the 19th I was in the Nile tavern, and the prisoner came and left 5 l. with me—he came to me on the Thursday following, and got it back.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known him? A. Fifteen or sixteen years—I never heard anything dishonest of him.
MARY ANN MAYHOOH . I live at Woolwich. On Monday after the 19th of Oct. the prisoner left 4 l. with me—I returned it him again afterwards.
(Henry Kebble, a reporter, living in Bell's-buildings; William Dyer Webb, a smith, living at Woolwich; and James Parry, a policeman, of Woolwich, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 32.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Six Months .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

167. ROBERT JAMES and ISABELLA COLLIS were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of Nov., 11 1/2 lbs. weight of beef, value 5 s., the goods of Robert Warth, the master of James; to which
JAMES pleaded GUILTY .— Confined Four Days .
ROBERT WARTH . I am a butcher, and live in East-lane, Greenwich. James was in my service—this beef produced is mine—it was in my salting-tub—I know it is mine.
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant R 15.) On the 19th of Nov., at a quarter past six o'clock, I saw Collis take this beef out of a pail—she lives next door to Mr. Warth—James brought the beef out in a pail, and placed it against Collis's door, and she came out directly, and took it out—it was within sight of Mr. Warth's premises—she took it directly—as soon as the bale of the pail fell, she came and took it out—she is married, and keeps a pork-shop and eating-house.
Collis. The beef was left there; he was to come for it again.

168. SARAH ANN WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of Nov., 4lbs. weight of pork, value 2 s., the goods of John Buckley; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN BUCKLEY . I am a pork-butcher, and live in Trafalgar-road, Greenwich. On the 9th of Nov., in the evening, I placed some pork on a board—I heard something, and missed a piece of pork—it was produced to me, and it was mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What time was it? A. Between six and seven o'clock—it was brought back in about five minutes after I missed it—it could be taken off the board as a person went by—I did not see it go—I was told of it by Patterson.
ELIZABETH PATTERSON . I live in East-lane. Between six and seven o'clock that evening I saw the prisoner go and push the meat off the board, and take it up.
Cross-examined. Q. You could not see what it was? A. I could not see that it was pork, but it was meat—I was at the top of the court where I live—I am a widow, and work at washing—I do not rent a house—the prisoner appeared the worse for liquor.
REGINALD WAPSHOTT ( police-constable R 259.) I took the prisoner with the pork under her arm.
Cross-examined. Q. What time was it? A. Between six and seven o'clock—I have not said it was between seven and eight—the prisoner was the worse for liquor.
MICHAEL THOMAS CANNON ( police-constable R 157.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's
office—(read—"Convicted on the 5th of Feb., 7th Vict., and sentenced to three months' imprisonment."
GUILTY .— Confined Nine Months .

[trialAccount t18441125-169]
169. JOHN GABRIEL was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of Nov., 1 waistcoat, value 12 s.; and 1 pair of trowsers, 16 s.; the goods of James Stevens, in a vessel on the navigable river Thames.
JAMES STEVENS . I am a seaman on board the Drdalus —she is now gone to sea, but she was lying at Woolwich. I had a jacket and trowsers on board the Hebe, which was lying off the Dock-yard—these are them—being on shore I did not go on board to put my things up, but to the best of my knowledge I saw these things safe on the Friday, as they were gone on the Saturday—I know the prisoner well—I took him on suspicion before I knew he had taken the things—he had been a messmate in the same berth with me in the Hebe.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. How many other messmates were there? A. Ten—several persons came on board the vessel occasionally—there were two came to visit me, and had tea—I kept my bag sometimes in the mess, and sometimes I tied it to the stanchion, to keep it from the dust—I took the prisoner on suspicion—I went up before the Magistrate—he was dismissed, but sent a prisoner on board the ship—he was under the sentry's charge—I was not on board the vessel when Myers came on board—I was in the Dock-yard at work—I did not see him—there were two females who visited me—they were not in the habit of visiting me frequently, but they came once or twice, and they came on the Monday previous, but I lost things out of my bag on the Monday—I lost several other things—I am married, and have four children—the names of the two females who visited me are Mrs. White and Mrs. Watts—they are both married—the husband of one of them is at sea, and the other is a caulker in the Dock-yard—I gave an account of this before the Magistrate, and signed my name to some paper—this is my name to this deposition—this was read over to me before I put my name to it—to the best of my knowledge I stated that I had seen these things on the Friday morning or Friday evening, but there are other things, which have not been found yet—these are only a part of the things—the prisoner and I have had no quarrel—we have been the best of friends—I know Sheen, a Thames police-officer—he went with me to a Jew's house to buy a pair of trowsers—I have seen Sheen to-day.
Q. Have you ever made use of words to this effect, "that if the prisoner got off you would give him a slick that would send him to Guy's? A. I said I would send him below, or something to that effect—I might have used the word Guy's—I said I should like to have him on board the ship again—he came on shore with me on Saturday, the 16th, at twenty or twenty-five minutes after six o'clock—he was in my company—I left him then, and after that I went to a public-house, where he promised to meet me in a quarter of an hour—I went there and he was gone—I then went down to the Nile, and sat some time—he did not come, and I thought there was a screw loose somewhere—he had been on board of the vessel at work with the rope maker, and I had been on shore at work in die Dock-yard—he had nothing with him when he came on shore with me to my knowledge,
but it was dark—he might have had fifteen pairs of trowsers on without my knowing it—to the best of my knowledge he had a pair of sailor's trowsers on—I did not take notice whether he had a blue jacket on—I know he was not dressed as he is now—the things he has now on belong to me—it was my friendship to lend him them—I cannot say whether he had that shirt on or not.
JOHN MYERS . I am a salesman, and live at Woolwich. On Saturday evening, the 16th of Nov., I purchased these things of the prisoner between seven and eight o'clock.
Cross-examined. Q. What do you deal in? A. In silver, and clothes generally, with officers of the garrison—they sell their clothes to me—it was on the 16th the prisoner came, between seven and eight o'clock—sometimes we have a good many persons coming in, and sometimes none at all for hours—we have not many sailors come—it is quite out of the way for them—my shop is towards the barracks—I suppose I have no trowsers at all like these—I had never seen the prisoner before the 16th—it was on Tuesday the 19th that the policeman came to my shop to ask about these things—he did not search my premises—I was not in at all—I was called to Major Elgin's, and my boy was hanging up the things—these were before the door, and they came and took the things—as I was coming home I met the policeman and my boy going with these things—I heard that these had been stolen—I saw the prisoner before the Magistrate on the following Monday—no one came with the prisoner when he sold them—my boy, Barnard, was by when he did come—my boy has been with me about five months—I have a gas-light in the shop—we had just opened the shop—we close on Friday night and open on Saturday night—the prisoner bringing things that did belong to him I had no suspicion—if he had brought a watch or gold lace I should have said, "Sailors do not belong to gold lace"—the Magistrate asked me what dress the man had who brought the things—I said it was something of a lightish jacket and light trowsers, something like canvass—I said to him, "Why do you sell your trowsers, you wear a pair of light trowsers, these will be handy for you"—he said, "I am rather out of employment, and am short, but I shall soon get in a Government vessel"—the officer sent a note for me to go before the Magistrate—I had noticed one of the prisoner's cheeks appeared rather higher than the other, as if it were swollen—I did not describe him as having dark whiskers—I did not have any further conversation with the prisoner—I said to him, "Clothes are very cheap, I cannot give you I the price you want"—he said he bought them at Portsmouth.
MOSES BARNARD . I am in the service of Mr. Myers—I saw the prisoner soner at his shop—I am sure it was the prisoner—that was on Saturday evening, the 16th of Nov., about eight o'clock—he sold my master a waistcoat and trowsers—these are them.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you put any mark on the things? A. Not at the time he sold them, but I looked at the buttons—I saw them in the dock-yard, and I knew them by the buttons—my master has plenty more trowsers, but not sailors' trowsers—he does not deal much in sailors' things, but when the prisoner came he bought these of him—I cannot say that he has never bought of sailors before—I have lived with him five months—he has got blue waistcoats—I was standing at the door when the prisoner came—I was not in the shop—I did not hear what he said, but I know he sold the things—my master has told me not to listen to
what a person says to him—I am to stand before the door—I did not state that the prisoner had dark whiskers—I never raised my hand to my face and show that he had hair on his face, and it was black—I do not recollect lect Mr. Hodson—I had never seen the prisoner before the 16th—the prosecutor and the officer came afterwards and saw the waistcoat hanging before the door—they asked me for the trowsers, and then took me to the Dock-yard—I looked over the sailors in the yard, and did not see the prisoner—the officer then called all the sailors from the ship, and I saw the prisoner—he had been down below in the ship, and was called up—the officer did not show him to me—he told me to look round on all the sailors to see which he was—I do not think he said, when the prisoner came up, "Is not this the man?"—directly I saw the prisoner I said, "This is the man"—the officer was standing by—I will swear he did not ask me whether that was not the man.
COURT. Q. How many sailors did you see? A. About fifty—no one said, "Is not that the man," but to look round, and I said, "This is the man."
WILLIAM SHEEAY ( police-constable R 304.) I took the prisoner on board the Dædalus—the prosecutor pointed him out to me—the prosecutor and I had been in search of the clothes at pawnbrokers, and then we went. to Mr. Myers's shop and found the trowsers and waistcoat, which he gave into my charge—Barnard then went down with me to the yard—we went on board the Hebe hulk—I asked the master-at-arms where the hands were—presently the prisoner came up with eight or ten sailors more—I asked Barnard if he saw him amongst them—he said, "That is the man," pointing to the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the lieutenant there? A. Yes; he said nothing to me or to Barnett about the prisoner—there were six or eight seamen on board, and then the prisoner came up—I did not see him come up, but presently I turned round and saw him standing there, and I asked Barnard whether he could point out the man—I heard nobody tell him anything about him but myself—I will swear no one said, "Is that the man."
EMILY WATTS . I am the wife of William Watts, who is employed as a caulker at Woolwich Dock-yard—I know the prosecutor and the ship's company of the Hebe —I went on board last Monday week—I saw the prosecutor—he made me tea on board.
Cross-examined. Q. How often had you been on board? A. That was the first time—I have been once since then.
MR. WILDE called
HENRY HARVEY HODSON . I keep the Royal Mortar tavern at Woolwich. I have known the prisoner five years—he lived in my house—his character has been as good as any boy I know—he was at my house on Saturday evening, the 16th—I cannot tell what time it was—I know his clothing so well that I am positive he never was possessed of a light jacket—his sister lived as cook in my house a number of years—she married, and then this poor boy was thrown on the world, and he was in my house for weeks and weeks—I think he was in my house on Saturday night, the 16th, about eight o'clock—he had a dress on similar to what he has now—he had no light jacket—Myers's house is nearly a mile from my house.
COURT. Q. Do you know what trowsers he had on? A. I do not, but I believe they were those he has now—they were lightish, but are now, from wear, rather brown—he had on the same dress he has now—I saw him at my house about eight o'clock—he was in the kitchen with the servant—he has known the servant ever since he was a child—I have to pass the kitchen frequently, and the door is generally open—I saw him there two or three times within half-an-hour—I think when I first saw him it was a quarter or twenty minutes before eight o'clock—he was in the habit of coming to my house of an evening when he had permission to leave the vessel—I recollect the day the prisoner was at my house, from the circumstance of the accusation, and then I recollected it was on the Saturday, from a conversation that took place in my coffee-room on the same evening, which caused me to come out to ask a question in my bar, and then I saw the prisoner, and I think that was before eight o'clock—none of my servants are here.
JAMES CANNON . I am a labourer to Mr. Watts, the high-constable. I have known the prisoner about two years—he was always a very good character, and very honest—I remember seeing him on Saturday evening, the 16th of Nov., about half-past seven o'clock—he had a pair of canvas trowsers, painted brown, and a blue frock.
COURT. Q. Where did you see him? A. At the Coach and Horses, and from there he went to Mr. Hodson's—we had a couple of pots of beer together, and the prosecutor too—he had not left him then—we came back again to the Coach and Horses—we met Stevens there, and then we all went together to Whale's, and had some drink.
ELLEN WHALES . I live right opposite the Ship and Half Moon, at Woolwich; I am married. The prisoner has been with me, off and on, for about five years—he has been honest—he was at my house that Saturday evening about a quarter after nine o'clock—he had lightish trowsers on, and a blue frock, tucked inside his trowsers, the very same as he has on now.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
