[trialAccount t18441125-171]
171. JANE CATTERMORE was indicted for stealing 1 shawl, value 3 s.; and 13 yards of ribbon, 6 s.; the goods of John Buckland, her master.
JOHN BUCKLAND . I live in Crosby-row, Walworth-road; the prisoner has been in my service about six weeks. In consequence of some suspicion, I sent for a policeman—her box was examined in her presence—she unlocked locked it—I found a shawl of mine in it—in consequence of that I discharged her—she went away, leaving her box behind—on the following day I made a further search of her box—(I did not search further at first,
as my wife was in a delicate state of health)—the key of it was lying in the house—I then found another shawl, a silk dress, and two pieces of gauze ribbon—I know the ribbons to be mine by a private mark on them—the shawl I know to be my wife's from her wearing it—the prisoner's mother came with her next day to beg I would look over it, but having found these additional things, I gave her in charge—these are the things—the letter "S" is on one piece of ribbon, and the letter "L" with 'two strokes on the other—I am a dealer in these articles—the prisoner was not present when the ribbon was found; I had sent her away the day before—it was not in the power of any one to put anything into her box after she was gone—I had missed the ribbon long before—I had put the key on the mantel—shelf in the sitting-room—my wife and a little servant-girl had ac-cess to that room—they are not here—the prisoner offered no defence to the shawl being in her box—she said she took the shawl out of the closet in one of the upper rooms, and she said she was very sorry.
HENRY SCOTT ( police-constable P 241.) I took the prisoner into cus-tody on the prosecutor's charge—I went up with him, and saw the two pieces of ribbon—he took them out of the box, and handed them to me—the box was unlocked—the prisoner was not present—she was not in the house—I received the shawl from the prosecutor also.
Prisoner's Defence. I took the shawl out of the closet in the shopboy's bedroom, where there were some old things; I did not think it would be missed; the two pieces of ribbon I took out of the show-room, when I was cleaning it in the morning.
JOHN BUCKLAND re-examined. The shawl was taken from the upper room—it was not thrown by, as not to be used again—the value of the ribbon is about 6 s., as near as I can form an idea, and the shawl 3 s.—I have inquired as to her former character, and believe her to be led away by the vanity of dress—there was much more valuable property within her reach.
GUILTY. Aged 19.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor.— Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18441125-172]
172. MARY CRISP was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of Nov., 4 pairs of clogs, value 2 s. 6 d.; 2 candlesticks, 2 s.; 1 metal tap and key, 1 s.; 1 pair of snuffers, 2 d.; 1 extinguisher, 3 d.; 3 printed books, 6 d.; 1 nutmeg-grater, 2 d.; 1 towel, 6 d.; 1 pinafore, 6 d.; 1 neckerchief, 1 s.; 1 smelling-bottle, 3 d.; 2 watch-glasses, 6 d.; 1 handkerchief, 6 d.; 1 necklace, 4 s.; 1 teetotum, 2 d.; 1 iron box, 2 s.; 1 pair of sugar-clippers, 2 s.; 1 pair of spectacles, 6 d.; 1 tray, 6 d.; 1 napkin, 1 s.; 2 tin biscuit-cutters, 1 d.; 11 skewers, 6 d.; 2 ink-glasses, 6 d.; 1 ring, 3 s.; 1 breast-pin, 1 s.; 1 spoon, 2 s.; and 1 penknife, 3 d.; the goods of Frederick Barnes, her master.
FREDERICK BARNES . I am a watch and clock maker, and live in Blenheim-place, High-street, Peckham. The prisoner was in my service about a month—I had not been engaged in business at Peckham above a fortnight—I carry on business at Farningham, in Suffolk—the prisoner was about to leave me—in consequence of having missed two tea-spoons I required to see her boxes—she would not allow me to see them—I went out, called on somebody, and on my return I found the constable with the prisoner in custody—her box was with her—they were coming towards my house—I saw her box searched by the constable a my house, and found a
variety of shop goods in it, to the value of about 30 s.—she had been engaged with me and my apprentice in removing goods from the house into the shop—the box contained the articles stated—these now produced ✗ them—some of them have my marks on them—these candlesticks have—I did not miss them till the box was searched—these candlesticks I had from Birmingham—I had seen them perhaps about a week before they were taken, when I marked them—the prisoner and my apprentice assisted in removing them from the sitting-room into the shop—this box-✗ I missed from my stock—it is the same size and exactly corresponds with it, and is made by the same maker—I had such things as all these in my stock—my mark is not on this iron—the paper was taken off—I know the candlesticks to be mine, and I believe the rest to be mine—some have my mark and some have not.
WILLIUM HAMMOND ( police-constable P 90.) On Friday evening, the 15th of Nov., I was called by Mrs. Barnes into the shop—while speaking to her the prisoner came in—I heard Mrs. Barnes ask her why she took her box away—she said because she wanted to book her place to go by the eight o'clock buss to Charing-cross—she ran out at the door, down to where she had taken her box, about fifty yards from Mr. Barnes's—I followed—she took her box from there—I took her into custody as she was turning the things out of her box into the chandler's shop—I stopped her from doing so, put the things back into the box, and took her and the box to Mr. Barnes's—on getting there the things now produced were found in her box—she said she had bought some of them at two or three shops above there—I found this silver spoon in her hand, a little knife, and this necklace.
FREDERICK BARNES re-examined. I cannot swear to the spoon—it is such a one as I had in use—I know these beads—I missed some, and these match exactly with them.
Prisoner. My mistress would not pay me my wages; she told me she should not pay me a farthing; I went up stairs in an obstinate humour, and did put the things into my box, I own; she took me more than 100 miles from home.
MR. BARNES. I told her I did not think she ought to be leaving me as she did, but I was going to pay her just before this robbery was found out—she said she had no fault to find with my mistress or me, but she did not like Peckham—I did not turn her away—we should have been glad to have had her stop if she had done right.
Prisoner. My mistress told me I might help myself, for I should not have a farthing of wages; I had no fault to find with my master or mistress, but I did not like Peckham.
MR. BARNES. I have no reason to believe that my wife told her to help herself—my wife has money—I do not know that she always has money by her—I suppose she had sufficient, but I do not know how it was at that time—I understood the prisoner to bear a very good character when I took her—her friends are very respectable—I do not know what wages she was to have.
Prisoner. My last mistress gave me 3 l., but said she would give me 5 l. if I would stop; Mrs. Barnes said she would give me 6 l. a year if I would come up with them.
GUILTY. Aged 20.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor. — Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18441125-173]
173. GEORGE JOHN PAGE was indicted for stealing 4 feet of brass tubing, value 4 s. 6 d.; 2 iron drills, 1 s.; and 2 iron brackets, 2 s.; the goods of the London and South Western Railway Company, his masters.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE WILLIAMS ( police-constable V 97.) In the morning of last Saturday week I went with Dalby and Ratcliff to No. 56, Wickham-street, Vauxhall—Ratcliff went up stairs and brought the prisoner down—I staid below—I then asked him to accompany me up stairs while I searched his bedroom—I found nothing there—the prisoner said, "It is no use looking here, you will find nothing; if you don't look into the box that is down in the back room I will give you a sovereign"—I went down stain where Dalby and Ratcliff were placed, and found a box there—it was locked—I asked the prisoner if that was his box—he said it was—I asked if he would be so good as to unlock it—he said he had not got the key—I said I should break it open—I did so, and found three large pieces of brass tubing, which I produce, and some drills and a screw-wrench were taken out of the box by Dalby—I then took the prisoner into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. NEALE. Q. What was the exact hour when you searched his bed-room? A. Between seven and eight o'clock in the morning—when Ratcliff brought him down, I told him what I came for—I searched the lower part of the house—there were two rooms in the lower part—his wife was there, and two children, I believe—there was no lodger that I know of—I partly searched the, rooms—I had previously gone up stairs with the prisoner, and, searched his bedroom—I told him we had come to take, him into custody, for offering some brass tubing for sale, and we had come to search his house—he then said, "It is no use your searching here, you will not find anything; if you don't search the box that is down stairs I will give you a sovereign"—no one was present then but the prisoner and myself—the sovereign was not given to me—I had just done searching the room when he said those words—he was pre-sent when we searched below—I did not find any sovereign on his person, or in the house—the box was in the back room, which is a kitchen or workshop—I had been in the house about two minutes before I saw him coming down stairs.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long have you been an officer? A. Eight years and a half.
ROBERT RATCLIFF ( police-constable V. 26.) I accompanied Williams and Dalby to the prisoner's house—I went into the bedroom, found the pri-soner in bed, and took him into custody—under a carpenter's bench in his workshop I found two iron brackets—when I was taking him to the sta-tion, he said some person must have put the, brass tubes into his pocket—he did not tell me how they got into his box.
JASPER B. DALBY . I am superintendent of the South-Western Railway police—I know the prisoner—he was not employed last Saturday week on the railway—I accompanied the other two officers to his house on the day in question, and saw the articles found—>as Ratcliff was lifting the brass tubing out of the box, he said, "Ah, there it is, there it is, but I can't help it; if money will settle it, you shall have it; but I will bring no one else in besides myself."
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been superintendent on this railway? A. A year and ten months—I may have seen the prisoner at
work there, but there are so many men—I was present when the prisoner was asked for the key of the box—he said he had not got one—as Ratcliff was taking him out of the house he handed this key to his wife which I took charge of, and it fitted the box in question—I tried it.
JOHN LANGTHORN . I am one of the engineers employed at the South-Western Railway terminus, at Nine Elms—the prisoner has heen in the Company's service, I think, nearly three years, as a carpenter—the Company had such brass tubing as this produced, for the purposes of the engine—the prisoner used to work close to where this was put—it is part of the tubing introduced into the boiler, for the steam to pass through, by which notice is given of any eruption.
Cross-examined. Q. This is a patent of one of the makers of the loco-motive? A. Yes, all the locomotives have tubing, but not the same as ours—this is the last patent—I don't know whose patent it is—it may be used on other railways—I believe the prisoner has always borne a good character.
CHARLES BUSH . I am a smith. I made two iron brackets similar to these produced, for the prisoner, to put in a coke-wagon on the railway—to the best of my knowledge it was in Oct.—I never made more than two for the Company, to my knowledge.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been in the service? A. Six years—the prisoner has been there three years, and always bore a good character—there are no marks about these brackets by which I can swear to them—I cannot swear they are my work—I do not know how many hundred coke-wagons there are on the railway—there are nine smiths in the same shop with myself—I never saw any brackets of this description made—I have made other kinds—I cannot swear these are what I made in Oct.—they are very much like them, but there are many smiths besides myself that make them.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Have you ever seen any two brackets of that form manufactured at the railway, except the two you made? A. No, and they were to repair a coke-wagon with.
HENRY BAKER . I am a brazier, employed by the South-Western Railway Company, at Nine Elms. In Nov. I worked at 128 brass tubes to fit this gauge—the prisoner worked about twenty yards from me—I produce one of the tubes which I so prepared—the broken pieces produced, to the best of my knowledge, have been prepared by me to fit this gauge—( The witness here fitted the tubing to the gauge)—it fits this gauge—the mouths were made smaller to fit the fire-box of the engine—these three pieces at one time formed one entire piece, but the whole length is not here—it is altogether useless in this state, except as old brass—a whole one would cost about 15 s. or 16 s.
Cross-examined. Q. You do not swear to these pieces? A. No—I have been a year and ten months on this railway—I formerly worked on the Northern and Eastern—I have been accustomed to make these sort of tubes about seven years—I never made any of this exact kind before—they have come up within the last two years and a half—I have seen them before I came on this railway, but not of this size—there are different diameters—they are used on other railways, but not of this gauge—I could not speak to it without the gauge—if tubing of the same gauge used on other railways was produced, I could not tell it from this.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had the prisoner anything to do with your brass rods? A. Not the least.
JOHN DOWLINO . I am foreman of the locomotive department. These two drills bear the initials of the London and South-Western Railway on them.
Cross-examined. Q. Did the prisoner come from the Northern and Eastern Railway Company? A. Yes—he has been a very attentive servant, and everything I could wish him to be.
GUILTY. Aged 32.— Strongly recommended to mercy.
Confined Two Months .
[trialAccount t18441125-174]
174. JAMES JOHNSON was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Samuel John Weldon, about the hour of five in the night of the 13th of Nov., at Bermondsey, with intent to steal.
SAMUEL JOHN WELDON . I live in Bermondsey-road, in the parish of Bermondsey—I occupy the whole house. I was disturbed in the night of the 13th of Nov., by the barking of my dog—I got up, opened the door, and listened—I did not find the staircase-window open then—I did afterwards, about half-past three o'clock—I immediately sprang my rattle, and let the police in at the front door—I found the sash of the kitchen window broken, also four panes of glass—there was room for a man to get in—the sash frame was carried out with the glass, and I found some marks of blood on the window-blind, such as might occur from a person's hand being cut—I am in the habit of keeping the staircase window always closed, and I know of no alteration in that respect that night—I did not notice that it was fastened, but it was decidedly down—some flowerpots which had been at the staircase-window, I found removed into the garden, as if they had been handed down—I lost no property—it appeared to me that the parties had entered by the staircase-window, and gone out by the kitchen-window.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. How did that appear? A. By the broken sash in the kitchen, and the staircase window was half open when I sprung my rattle—if the man had entered at the front, he must have made a noise, and I should have heard it—the kitchen window is at the front—I did not hear him go out—I did not hear the windows opened—my name is Samuel John—my usual name is John—I have not used the name of Samuel for a long time—few persons know me by that name—my wife does, but no one else, I apprehend—I was never called Samuel as a child—the name was given me, I believe, by my godfathers—I never knew myself called by that name—I never sign my name Samuel, only John—I know I was christened Samuel, because I have seen my baptismal certificate—I have never used the name in matters of importance, where I have had to sign my name correctly—I did not use it when I was married—my godfathers have been dead many years—it is nearly sixty years ago that I first heard that my name was Samuel—I have seldom or ever had the name attributed to me since, in fact, never, to my knowledge—it was my baptismal certificate that informed me my name was Samuel—I have not got it with me—I can swear that my name is Samuel—I have not examined the register of baptisms—my father left no will, nor is there any family document in which I am recognised as Samuel—I have never signed the name of Samuel—I mentioned it here, in consequence of being asked if I had more than one name—I
mentioned John only, as I have been in the constant habit of signing every document in that name.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18441125-175]
175. DANIEL CONNER was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Oct., 2 sheets, value 7 s.; 3 shirts, 9 s.; 1 shift, 2 s.; 1 pair of stocking, 1 s.; 1 towel, 1 s.; and 1 copper, 10 s.; the goods of John Gault, the copper being fixed to a certain building: 2nd COUNT, not stating it to be fixed.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
MARY GAULT . I am the wife of John Gault, who is in the employ of the Brighton Railway Company—we live at No. 35, in the Maze, Borough. On the 2nd of Oct. I hung out some clothes in my yard, and saw them safe at nine o'clock at night—I fastened the back door, and at that time the copper was safe in the back-kitchen—the door was only latched—the policeman brought the clothes to me at near two o'clock in the morning—they they were the articles stated, and are all my husband's property—the prisoner was then in custody—I missed the copper—it had been fixed with mortar and bricks—it has since been refixed.
THOMAS CROCKETT . I am a salesman in the Borough-market, and live at No. 36, in the Maze, next door to the prosecutor. On the 24th of Oct., I was disturbed about half-past one o'clock in the morning, and beard a something rumbling in the passage between my house and the prosecutor's—it sounded to me like copper—I got but of bed, threw up the window, and saw a man who had just passed my window a little to the left—I then saw a second person, the prisoner, come out of the passage—I hallooed out, "Halloo, mate, what is up there?"—he made no answer—I sprang my rattle, and the policeman flew to the corner—the prisoner was met by the police in less than five minutes, and brought back.
COURT. Q. Had you ever seen him before? A. Not to my knowledge—I did not see him taken, but saw him brought back in five minutes—I am sure he is the person I saw come out of the passage.
GEORGE TOMPKINS , ( police-constable M 166.) On the night of the 23rd of Oct. I heard a rattle spring—I went towards the sound, and met the prisoner in Tooley-street, just at the end of the Maze, not a great distance from the passage—he was carrying a bundle, containing sheets, and other linen, wet—I stopped him, and took him back to Mrs. Gault's house—she identified the articles—most of them have been returned to her—as I was taking him back, Crockett was at his window, and identified him in a moment.
GEORGE ADAMS ( police-constable M 156.) I was on duty in Tooley-street on this night—I heard the rattle spring, and went to the spot—in consequence of something I heard, I looked for the copper, and found it in a mews, right opposite the prosecutor's, concealed under a block or washing stool—Mrs. Gault identified it—I did not fit it.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming home about half-past one o'clock; I live in the same street; a young man asked me if I would carry a bundle from the Maze; I said I would; he gave it me, and told me to go on, and when I got to the top of the Maze the policeman stopped me, and said, "What have you got?" I said, "I don't know; I have just had it given me to carry."
GUILTY of stealing only.** Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18441125-176]
176. WILLIAM DIXON was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of Nov., 1 breast-pin, value 3 l., the goods of William Hughes.
WILLIAM HUGHES . I am a jeweller, and live at No. 117, High-street, Southwark. The prisoner came to my shop on Tuesday, the 19th of Nov., to examine some pins—he looked at some diamond pins, and selected one, on which he left 1 s. deposit, stating that his brother would call in the morning and pay the difference—the price of that pin was 2 l. 12 s.—after he had left the shop we looked over the cushion and discovered a pin of the value of 35 s. missing—he came again the following morning to look at some more diamond pins—my suspicions were naturally aroused, and I counted the number, which was eighteen, and placed them on a cushion before him—during the time a customer came into the shop, and entered into conversation—the prisoner also entered into conversation with him—while the gentleman was in conversation with me, the prisoner took one diamond pin from off the cushion, and secreted it up his sleeve—I saw him—immediately after he had done so, he called my attention to one of the pins which he had previously been looking at, and wished me to send that pin to his brother's house—he particularly wished I should bring it myself with the pin on which he had left the deposit the day before—I took down the address which he gave me, "No. 5, Mary Ann-place, Southward bridge-road," which I afterwards discovered to be a fictitious address—I counted the pins after I had seen him put the one up his sleeve, and there were only seventeen—while taking the address down, I rang the bell for my assistant—the prisoner still had the diamond pin up his sleeve—I could not see it up his sleeve, but I am quite positive he put it there—I called my assistant twice after that, and while calling him the prisoner dropped the pin from his sleeve on to a ledge on the counter—I am quite positive of that, and I am quite positive the pin had not been there previous to my calling my assistant, for I bad distinctly seen the spot where the pin was afterwards found, and there was nothing on it—I did not hear the pin fall—I charged him then with having taken the pin, and also one the day before, and I said in a hurried manner, "If you will give me back the one you took the day before, I will not send for a policeman—he denied it, and I immediately sent for a policeman, and gave him into custody—I went to No. 5, Mary Ann-place, and they knew no such person as Mr. Henry Dixon—the landlord said a Mr. V. Jay did live at the house, but no Mr. Henry Dixon—the prisoner had said I was to take them to Mr. V. Jay, and ask for Mr. Henry Dixon, who was his brother—I have nobody here from that house.
Prisoner. Q. Who was in the shop the second day I went there? A. My assistant when you first came in—a gentleman came in while you were there; he looked at the pins on the cushion, made some remarks on the colour of the stones, and took them to the door to look at them, but the one you put up your sleeve was not then on the cushion—I will swear he never touched that pin—what pins the gentleman looked at were taken off the cushion—this one was on the cushion—the gentleman had not the opportunity of taking the pin as well as you—there were eighteen pins after he left—he could have removed the pin certainly, but he did not—he left before you—I said if you would restore me the pin you had stolen the day before, or its value, I would not give you into custody—I counted the pins when I took them out of the window and put them before you—I
had time to do so—they were placed one, two, three, four, and five, so that I could count them—I counted them five or six times while on the counter, expecting every time to miss one—when you had put the pin up your sleeve I had an opportunity of looking along the ledge where you afterwards placed it—I could see it was not there—you were going out, and I was anxious to prevent it—I could not see the ledge when I was behind the counter—the gentleman had left the shop before you put the pin there—I did not see you put it there—I saw you put it up your sleeve—I did not count the pins at the time I took them out of the window the first day, or I should have discovered it—it was the second day—there was eighteen—I did not count them the first day, but one particular pin marked 35 s. was gone—that is how I know it was missing—the depositions were not taken down in Court before you on Wednesday—when you were taken up I asked Mr. Cottingham to be kind enough to remand you that I might go round to the trade, thinking I might discover some other case, and I found you had taken two pins from Mr. Hayes, of Farringdon-street, and had only come out of prison four days—the officer from Giltspur-street gave me that information—I counted my pins at the time I gave you into custody, and told you there were only seventeen—no pin was found on you—I saw you place the pin up your sleeve—I did not see it when it was up there.
GEORGE TOMKINS ( police-constable M 166.) I received charge of the prisoner at the prosecutor's shop, with the pin he was charged with secreting ing there—I have it here—I went to the address the prosecutor represented the prisoner had given, and found no such name as Dixon known there.
Prisoner's Defence. I would first call your attention to the manner in which the prosecutor acts towards me when he accuses me of felony. He first endeavours to extort money from me by threatening to give me in charge for felony, and because I would not compromise the matter for 35 s. he gives me in custody. He says himself he offered to compromise it; surely you cannot believe that that man comes here for justice, for he offers to compromise the matter; does he want justice, or to compromise justice? if he is a respectable man, and no doubt he is, as the keeper of a jeweller's shop, does it show respectability on his part to attempt to compound a felony? I know nothing of the law myself, or perhaps he would be liable to punishment for it. I shall leave it in your hands. My friends are respectable and monied persons, but I did not wish them to know where I was, or to have my name in the public papers, which induced me to give a false name when I was taken up. I can refer to Davis and Company, furriers, Bull's Head Court; they know my father very well. I wrote to my father, saying that I was in difficulty, but have not let him know where I am. I throw myself on the mercy of the Court. I fully expect if I am brought in guilty, that I shall be transported; the officers of Newgate have many of them told me so.
GUILTY.* Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years .
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.

178. SARAH HEELAN was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying James Lonergan.
MR. CURWOOD conducted the Prosecution.
MARY KENNEDY . I live in Mulgrave-place, Woolwich. I have known the prisoner for about the last four or five months—I live in the room over her—she has two sons, and had a little boy named Lonergan in her care—on Wednesday, the 16th of Oct., I remember that little boy coming down to me—he then appeared well in health—he asked me for a light and a drink of water—I opened the door to let him out of my washhouse—he appeared in good health.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When did the child die? A. I cannot exactly say—I never saw it dead—the last time I saw him was on Thursday, the 17th, about five o'clock—Mrs. Mead brought him up in her arms, and I was frightened at its appearance—I did not observe that it had any eruption—I turned my head away and did not see its face—the prisoner had always been very kind to the child, and kept it very comfortable—she was very attentive to her own children, and to that too for the last four weeks—she treated it with equal kindness as her own children.
JAMES PARRY ( police-sergeant R 8.) I took the prisoner into custody on the 19th of Oct.—I first spoke to her son, who is eight or nine years old, in her presence, and said, "Show me where your mother hit little Jemmy's head"—the child went to the door with me to show me where the child was struck—the prisoner, in two or three minutes, said, "Here is where it was"—she took me into the room and said, "The child fell off this box," which was a box about eighteen inches high.
COURT. Q. Was it possible to have fallen from that box and struck its head against the corner of the box? A. Yes—the child pointed out the door as the place against which its head was struck—the door is not a foot from the box—the prisoner said it had fallen off the box, and struck its head against the corner—that might have been so if there was only one injury on the head, but there were three—after taking her to the station-house some friends came to see her, and as she returned to the cell she sighed and said, "Oh, poor little fellow! he hit his head against the iron pot."
( MR. CURWOOD here stated that he had no evidence to contradict the prisoner's statement, and declined calling further witnesses.)
NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
