180. ELLEN M'CARTY, alias Horne , was indicted for uttering counterfeit coin, having been previously convicted of a like offence.
MR. CHAMBERS conducted the Prosecution.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . I am assistant solicitor to the Royal Mint. I produce a copy of the record of the conviction of Ellen M'Carty at this Court, in the August Session, 1848—( read.)
EDMUND MATTHEWS . I was a police-constable. In August, 1848, I attended in the New Court, and was present at the prisoner's trial for uttering three counterfeit shillings—I know her well.
Prisoner. He knew me before I was used to anything of the kind, and spoke in my favour before. Witness. It is twelve or fifteen yean ago since I knew her, and seven or eight years ago since I missed her.
SARAH COSNIGHT . My husband is a coaldealer, and lives in Mason-street, Lambeth. On Saturday evening, the 16th of Nov., between eight and nine o'clock, the prisoner came and ordered a 1/4 cwt. of coals, which would come to 4 3/4 d., and told me to send them to John-street—she did not give me the number—I do not know of any John-street in the neighbourhood—she gave me a half-crown in payment—I discovered it was bad immediately, and handed it over to my husband—she was takes into custody immediately.
Prisoner. She handed the half-crown to a woman who came in, and asked her opinion of it—she then gave it to her husband, and he said it was bad. Witness. It never went out of my hand, only to my husband—I never asked anybody's opinion on it.
RICHARD COSNIGHT . I received the half-crown from my wife—I kept it till Daviet, the officer, came and took her into custody, and then gave it to him.
JOHN DAVIES ( police-constable L 46.) I took the prisoner—Mr. Cosnight gave me the half-crown, which I produce—the prisoner was searched by a female.
JOHN HATCHETT . I am a green-grocer, and keep a coal-shed in Royal-street, Lambeth—on Friday night, the 8th of Nov., about a quarter to nine o'clock, the prisoner came and ordered a 1/4 cwt. of coals to be sent to No. 14, Hosier-street—I said there was no such street, she most mean Crozier-street—she said she did—the coals came to 4 3/4 d.—she gave me half-a-crown in payment—I gave her the change, and she went away—I took the coals to No. 14, Crozier-street, and found none had been ordered, nor any such person as the prisoner known there—she gave me no name—I then looked at the half-crown, and found it was bad—I chopped it up, and threw it away—I am certain it was the one the prisoner gave me—I had no other.
Prisoner. Q. Are you sure I am the person? A. I am quite sure-Goff, the policeman, did not tell me to recognise you—I recognised you✗ before he spoke to me—he did not tell me I should be well paid for my trouble.
DINAH HAYNES . I am a coal-dealer, and live in Little Surrey-street. On Saturday, the 9th of Nov., between six and seven o'clock in the evening, ing, the prisoner came and ordered 1/4 cwt. of coals, to go to No. 10, Union-street—they came to 4 1/2 d., she gave me a half-crown, and I gave her 2 s. 1 1/2 d. change—I kept the half-crown in my hand, and directly she had left the shop I found it was bad, from the feel—I weighed the coals directly, and took them and the half-crown to No. 10, Union-street, but no coals had been ordered there—I gave Lawrence the same half-crown—I had no other.
Prisoner. I never saw this woman till I saw her at Union-hall
Witness. I am sure she is the person—I had never seen her before—she was a few minutes in my company—I hare no doubt of her.
SAMUEL THOMAS LAWRENCE . Mrs. Haynes save me a half-crown—I showed it to Mrs. Saunders, and afterwards to Mr. Price—he compared it with a good one, marked it, and returned it to me—I saw him mark it on the edge with a good half-crown—he rubbed it—I gave it to Sergeant Brown at the station—this now produced is it—here is the mark—it is the one I received from Mrs. Haynes.
Prisoner. That is not the evidence he gave against me at the office.
Witness. After I received it from Mrs. Haynes I put it into a quart pot, and left it there all night—I took it out again in the morning, and when Mr. Price called in I showed it to him, and said, "Here is a good half-crown for you"—he said, "It is a very good one, I will give you change for it"—I said, "You had better be careful"—he looked at it, and sounded it—he looked at it for about ten minutes, and would not believe it was a bad one—he then took a good half-crown, and rubbed the edge of the other against it—he then said it was bad—I am certain this is the same—I had marked it with a pencil before I put it into the quart pot, the the mark was on it when I took it out in the morning—it has been rubbed out since.
Prisoner. He did not mention about the pencil-mark at Union-hall.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint. This half-crown, uttered to Mrs. Cosnight, is counterfeit, also the one just produced, and both have been cast in the same mould.
Prisoner's Defence. I leave myself to the mercy of the Judge and Jury. I can assure you I am not guilty of giving the half-crown that was put into the quart pot.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years .
Before Mr. Justice Maul✗
[trialAccount t18441125-181]
181. SAMUEL SIMS was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Edward Everest the elder: he was also charged on the Coroner's Inquisition with the like offence.
MESSRS. BALLANTINE and WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN PRIOR . I am a labourer, and live at No. 152, Rotherhithe-street. On the Sunday morning I found the body of Edward Everest—I took him ashore, and delivered him into the hands of his friends and the beadle.
EDWARD EVEREST . I am an inspector of the Thames police—the deceased wan my father—he was a waterman, and had been so many years—he was sixty-six years of age. I was on the Surrey shore on the 25th of Oct., about ten minutes before ten o'clock in the morning, and saw him leave the Surrey shore about thirteen or fourteen minutes before ten, with four persons in a skiff with him—it was a boat of such a size as he could manage properly—I was standing on the causeway at Church-stairs, Rotherhithe—the tide was nearly half flood—this was in the Pool—after he had left I noticed the Waterman steamer, No. 6—it was coming up with the tide, and going at the rate of eight or nine miles an hour, in my opinion—the proper rate for steamers navigating the Pool is about five miles an hour—the Waterman is a Woolwich boat—at the time I first saw the Waterman coming up, my father was, I should say, better thun two-thirds across the water, and about 150 yards from the steamer—I lost sight of my father's boat on my attention being called to the steamer—there was a tier of vessels between my father's boat and the steamer—there were about six
vessels in the tier at that time—there were no vessels whatever obstructing the channel of the river—the steamer seemed to be making a straight course for the Upper Tunnel-pier—that is not a slanting course from one pier to another—they take their course from below the Lower Tunnel-pier, in general—supposing she was coming from there, she would have to cross the river—she was going straight towards the Upper Tunnel-pier from where I saw her first, which was off King Edward's-stairs—that is, at the lower side of the Lower Tunnel-pier—the Tunnel stairs are on the Middlesex side—when I first saw the steamer, her bows were slanting towards the Tunnel-pier—King Edward's-stairs are lower down the river than the Tunnel-pier, and on the Middlesex side—I observed the Waterman No. 6 making a very heavy careen on one side, leaning on one side, and I observed a skiff come under her stern momentarily afterwards, and saw three or four persons struggling in the water from the skiff—that was just above the Lower Tunnel-pier—they were going towards the Upper Tunnel-pier—there are two Tunnel piers, which are near about 100 yards apart—there was a schooner moored just above the Lower Tunnel-pier—I did not observe the position of that schooner either then or afterwards—I went off in my own boat, came up to the skiff, and found it was my father's.
Q. After seeing this careen, did you notice anybody on the paddle-box? A. I did—it was the prisoner—it is usual to have a person on the paddle-box—he box—he directs the motions of the vessel—it is his duty to give the steersman a signal which way he wishes the vessel to go—I saw my father's body on Saturday morning—his name was Edward Everest—there was no diminution of the speed of the steamer before this careen.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What is the name of the stairs that are close in on the Lower Tunnel-pier? A. Wapping Dock-stairs—I believe those were the stairs my father was going to—I did not see the schooner till after the accident—Wapping Dock-stairs are very close to the Lower Tunnel-pier, and a boat going across the river to Wapping Dock-stairs would be shut in by the pier after she had got to a certain extent—if anything projected beyond the pier she would be more shut in.
JOHN SHADRACK . I am a seaman, formerly of the Valiant. On Friday, the 25th of Oct., I got into a boat on the Thames—there were five in the boat altogether with the person who was rowing—I did not know him—we were going over to Wapping Dock-stairs—as we were going along we met with a barge going up along the tide—I sang out, "Waterman, here is a barge coming," and he rowed astern of it—after that we met a schooner, and we went ahead of the schooner—after we had passed the schooner we saw a steamer coming up—we were sufficiently near to see the number—it was No. 6, Waterman —we were between 100 and 150 feet from the steamer, as near as I could judge—she was coming up along the tide—when I saw her, I stood up and waved my hand for them to keep more out into the middle of the river, which they did not—she did not make the least alteration in her course after I made that sign—I saw a person on the paddle-box—I do not know who it was—he saw me wave my hand—he had an opportunity of altering his course so as to keep more into the middle of the river—when the waterman saw the steamer coming he rowed as hard as he could—the steamer's stem nearly caught the boat, and knocked the scull out of the waterman's hand—he rowed round with his right hand, so that the steamer should not strike the boat right in the middle, so as to slant the boat along the steamer, and the fore part of the paddle-box struck
the fore part of the boat, turned her over, and we were all upset in the water—two lives were lost, and nearly the whole—as near as I could judge, the steamer was going at full speed, at the rate of ten miles an hour—I have been employed at sea nearly eight years—I can partly judge of the speed at which a boat is going, not exactly—I did not see any schooner.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Are you acquainted with steam-boats at all? A. No—I have not been much on the river.
JAMES MAYLOR . I am a seaman. I was on board this skiff on the 25th of Oct.—I saw the steamer coming up—at that time there was nothing between us and the steamer—she was coming at nine or ten knots an hour—I saw Shadrack get up and make a sign—there was a man on the paddle-box at that time—if he had put his helm a-starboard the least bit it would have cleared us—it came up to us—there was no lessening of speed that I noticed—the boatman pulled his right-hand oar—that was the proper course to adopt, or else the steamer would have cut the boat right in the middle—the stem of the paddle-box struck the boat, and it was overturned—I can swim—I attempted at first to swim to the steamer, but did not do so, because I saw that she was not stopping—her paddles were still in motion, but they might have been eased a little—I did not notice the schooner moored to the Lower Tunnel-pier, and have not seen it since—our skiff was not drawn by any current caused by anything near the shore—it was in no current at all, except the tide.
Cross-examined by SIR JOHN BAYLEY. Q. You say Shadrack hailed the steamer? A. He waved his hand—I did not hear the steamer hail us till she was close on us, and then they sang out, "Hoy"—that was just as the collision happened—it was too late then—we were about two or three barges' length from Wapping Dock-stairs, as near as I could judge.
THOMAS PALMER . I am a blacksmith. On Friday, the 25th of Oct., I was one of the four persons who were going over to Wapping Dock-stairs, in the boat rowed by the deceased—we saw the steam-boat coming up about 150 feet from us—a sailor, who was in the boat, stood up, and halloed out several times, and waved his hand several times—no notice was taken—the course of the steamer was not at all altered, nor the speed slackened—she struck our skiff, and we were all upset—the wheels of the steamer were in motion at the time the collision took place.
JURY. Q. Are you sure they were turning the usual way, and not turned the other way? A. No, they were turning towards our boat—I am quite lure of that.
Cross-examined by SIR JOHN BAYLEY. Q. Did you see the steamer give a sudden careen? A. Yes—that did not alter her course—I saw her coming up as fast when she struck our boat, as when I first saw her—my jaw was broken in three places on this occasion, and it hurts me very much now—the steamer went in towards the shore—she did not hail us—I did not see any one on board hail us—I heard somebody call out, "Stop her;" but whether that came from the steamer or any small boat I cannot say—I heard that when she was about twenty or thirty feet from us—I was before the Coroner—there were some small boats on the river, and the cry of "Stop her" might have proceeded from them—I am sure it did not come from the steamer—( looking at his deposition)—this is my name and handwriting.
( The witness's deposition being read, stated, "When within ten or twenty feet of the steamer I heard some one on board that boat say, 'Stop her,'
&c. I thought the words 'Stop her' proceeded from the steamer, but I cannot tell.")
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you see the schooner ashore at the Lower Tunnel-pier? A. No.
WILLIAM JEFFORD . I am a waterman. On Friday, the 25th of Oct., I was on the Lower Tunnel-pier, and saw the Waterman, No. 6, coming up the river—she was going at the rate of eight or nine miles an hour—I do not know whether that is her full speed or not—there was a schooner moored to the Lower Tunnel-pier—I had moored her there some time before—I saw a skiff and a man rowing—the steam-boat struck it—she was not above her own length from the skiff when I first saw her—she was just on the point of striking—I did not see whether it had at all altered its direction before it struck the boat—the skiff was twenty-one feet long—the speed of the steamer was not diminished to my sight—I had seen the steamer before—at the time I saw the skiff the steamer was going in the same direction as she had been before—she had not altered her course from the time I first saw her—when I first saw her she was about 150 feet from where I stood—it was not half a minute between my first seeing her and her striking the skiff—I have been acquainted with the river for some time.
Q. What is, in your judgment, the proper rate for going in the Pool? A. Four miles against the tide, I think it is; and six miles with it—I dare say the tide itself would drive a barge along at the rate of four miles an hour, with a person just now and then to straighten her or turn her round—in my judgment there ought to be an increase of only two miles an hour put on by the steamers—the schooner that I had moored caused some eddy; but at the time the skiff was overturned she had not come within the influence of that eddy.
Cross-examined by SIR JOHN BAYLET. Q. Are you employed at the Lower Tunnel-pier? A. Yes—the "Waterman" boats do not run to that pier—I did not see the steamer starboard her helm—she might have done so, in the course of the way she was going—I think she ought to have starboarded her helm, to have cleared the boat—I saw the schooner aground—she caused a little eddy above her—she caused more eddy when she was aground than when she was lifted—a good many steamers come to the Lower Tunnel-pier, as many as to the other—they travel at the same speed as the others—there is very little difference in any of them—one is as good as the other—there is never above a minute difference in speed in any of them—sometimes one is first, and sometimes the other is first.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. I suppose you are only employed as a servant at this pier? A. That is all—I have no share or interest in the boats—I think if the helm had been starboarded, it would have enabled the steamer to avoid the boat.
JOHN CHAMBERS . I am a master mariner, belonging to the schooner Ann —on Friday, the 25th of October, she was lying at the Tunnel pier—whilst there I saw the skiff and steam-boat, and saw them come into collision—the steam-boat was going, I should think, at about eight miles an hour, as near as I could judge, when she was passing our ship—when the boats came together I was, I should think, between eighty and ninety feet from them.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You were on board the schooner when this happened? A. Yes, on the quarter-deck—I had run ashore that morning—at the time in question my vessel was just lifted
astern, but was hard and fast by the head—her stern projected about ten feet beyond the Lower Tunnel-pier, as near as I can calculate—there were ropes made fast to the Tunnel pier—she is one hundred tons burden—her canvass was all struck and stowed—her boom projected about four feet over the stern—it is a smooth stern—we went there about high water about three o'clock in the morning, from some accident—we were going to discharge, and another vessel came and took her anchor up—about nine or ten in the morning we were lying with her stern right athwart the stream—I observed the steamer come up—I did not see the skiff till she was close to the steam-boat—she was about seventy or eighty feet from us, higher up the river, between the Upper and Lower Tunnel-pier, inside of us—there was a very strong eddy created by the position in which my schooner lay across the tide.
Q. Would the effect of that eddy, on a small boat, coming from the southward to the northward, if it got into it, be to carry it out again into the stream, instead of enabling it to go to Wapping Dock-stairs? A. Oh yes, and it would affect a ship as well as a small boat—I was in it no later than this passage up—if it had not been for the eddy I think the skiff would have made the stairs easily enough—I saw the skiff when she turned round in the eddy, towards the steam-boat—that was the effect of the eddy on her—that eddy would not be perceivable to a vessel coming straight up the river, not till she got past us—the schooner's stem would shut out the appearance of the eddy, all the way above it.
Q. Then, in your judgment, from the effect the eddy had on the skiff, could the accident have been avoided by the commander of the steamer? A. I think the accident was all occasioned by the eddy—if the boat had not turned round towards the steamer, I think she would have cleared it—the eddy was the occasion of turning it round—I do not think the master of the steamer could have avoided the accident—he could not have perceived the eddy until he came past us—I did not observe the helmsman starboard the helm when the small boat was seen—I could not see any negligence or carelessness on the part of the captain—I am in no way interested in steam-boats—they are as much injury to me as they are to any person—if the small boat was coming in the direction that has been stated, the effect of the eddy would be to catch her head, and cant her round, and saw that
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You say you think the master of the steamer could not have avoided it? A. Not from the time of my seeing it—they were that close together when I saw them, that he could not have avoided it—I should think they were not more than the length of the boat apart—I think there was—not then sufficient time to back the steamer—if she had been coming at the rate of but five or six miles an hour, I do not think it could have been avoided, they were too close together when I saw them—they were almost in the act of striking—there is, perhaps, about 200 fathoms' breadth between the Tunnel-pier and the tier of vessels on the other side of the river, but the steamer was coming to the pier where she was going to land her passengers.
JURY. Q. Would the eddy have any effect on vessels seventy or eighty feet from yours? A. Yes—straight upwards, but not outside—the tide is very strong at spring-tides.
COURT. Q. Was the eddy anything extraordinary, or is it a thing
which commonly happens when a vessel is stuck out into the tide and across it? A. Yes, it commonly happens—a person accustomed to the river, on seeing a schooner there, would know that there must be an eddy, if they saw the vessel was aground, if they took notice of it.
RICHARD FIELD . I am a waterman, employed by the Tunnel-pier Company at the Lower Tunnel-pier. On the 25th of Oct. I saw the Waterman, No. 6, coming up before ten o'clock in the morning—I saw a skiff with persons in it—it was about forty or fifty yards from the steamer when I first saw it—I saw some man in the boat, get up and wave his hand to the steamer.
Q. From the position of the steamer and the skiff at that time, could the steamer, in your opinion, have avoided it? A. Yes, if he had altered his helm, by starboarding the helm, or stopping the engine, might have saved it—there was nothing to prevent them taking a left-hand course—I saw a man on the paddle-box—if he had been looking out he must have seen the boat—he sung out to them, "Hoy, hoy"—he did not stop the steamer at all—he kept on his course—I saw an eddy from the schooner that was lying aground—that eddy could not have affected the skiff at all—she was away from it—she was in the tide's way—if she had been in the eddy, the steamer could not have got at her at all.
Cross-examined by. MR. CLARKSON. Q. What are you at the Lower Tunnel-pier? A. A waterman—I act for the Company—I am on duty there—these boats go to the Upper Tunnel-pier—when a steamer is going with the tide, by merely stopping her engine, and not turning it astern, she would go a long distance before she stopped still—perhaps she would go half a mile before she would stop by herself, so as to have only tide-way upon her, unless she was stopped by the engine being reversed—they could stop her by reversing the engine in her own length, that is my judgment—the length of the Waterman, No. 6, is eighty or ninety feet—if she was going at eight or nine knots, and the engine were reversed, I think she might be brought up in that distance—our steam-boats go at the rate of eight or ten miles an hour on the river.
Q. It is a rival company, is it? A. Yes; they are in conjunction now—they go to different piers—there is no opposition now—the two companies have agreed together.
JURY. Q. Were the companies in conjunction at the time of the accident? A. I think they were.
COURT. Q. Do you mean that these steamers go at ten miles an hour in the Pool? A. Yes; a steamer obeys the helm the better the quicker she is going—if she was only going with the tide she would not obey it at all.
Witnesses for the Defence.
ROBERT WILSON . I am a waterman. On Friday, the 25th of Oct., I was on the Thames in a wherry, a regular waterman's boat—I was coming from the Wapping Dock-stairs adjoining the Tunnel, about a quarter or twenty minutes before ten o'clock in the morning—I noticed a schooner aground against the Lower Tunnel-pier—that schooner created a great deal of eddy, and a very dangerous one, by lying across the tide—I was caught in the eddy, and it threw me out on a different course to what I wanted to go, and I bad to recover myself to go on my course—I regained my course, and landed my passengers—I returned in about ten minutes, and on my
return I was drawn by the eddy of this vessel up against the stern of the vessel with great violence—there was no one in my boat then—I was thrown out of my course both in going and returning—I knew of the eddy before I got into it.
MR. WILDE. Q. How long have you been a waterman? A. Very nearly thirty years—the effect of the eddy was to draw me up against the schooner—till I got clear of her stern, it would hug me to the vessel, but then the race of the tide would drive me from her—I was going from Wapping Dock-stairs across the river.
COURT. Q. Then, till you got beyond the end of the schooner you were protected from the tide running up, in some degree by the pier and schooner? A. Yes, by the eddy—the schooner was between me and the main run of the tide—when I got beyond the eddy the tide turned my head out—I had three passengers at first—neither of them are here—it had more effect on the boat then, but it acted differently as we were going a different way—the heavier the boat, the more power the eddy would have upon it—she would be deeper in the water, and the eddy runs quicker under the water than on the surface.
SIR JOHN BAYLEY . Q. When you were leaving Wapping Dock-stairs, the effect of the eddy was to throw your boat's head out into the river? A. Yes; and as I returned it drew me up against the vessel.
THOMAS JONES . I am a waterman lighterman. On Friday, the 25th of Oct., I was navigating a barge on the river—I was going to stop it at the Upper Tunnel-pier—I was going up with the tide—I saw a schooner aground against the Lower Tunnel-pier, which I belong to—that schooner caused an eddy, about twice the vessel's length, round her—it was up and down the river too—it caused a circular eddy—it ran right round, astern of the vessel in particular—she laid athwart the river—the eddy extended a good way out, about two barges' length from her into the river—my barge got into the eddy, and it stopped me from going to the place where I intended, the Upper Tunnel-pier—it turned her round twice—when I found she was in the eddy I let her go her own road, and put my oar in, finding it was no use—I began to feel the eddy soon after I came to the schooner, almost directly I came opposite her.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. The eddy was merely die course of the tide being obstructed? A. By the schooner—the eddy would certainly extend more than many inches beyond the extreme point of the vessel—the vessel lying aground on the causeway, stops the tide flowing its original course, and throws it right away—I saw nothing of the accident—it was about twenty minutes before ten o'clock when I came up to the schooner—the Pool was middling clear at that time—if I had not been caught by the eddy, my barge would not have been turned round—I was not in the midstream—I was about two barges' length from the schooner, and was going to stop at the Upper Tunnel-pier—I could not get to the Tunnel pier without going through the eddy.
WILLIAM MASON . I am collector for the Chartered Gas Company. On the morning of the 25th of Oct. I was standing on the Upper Tunnel pier, and saw the schooner aground, alongside the Lower Tunnel pier—I saw a skiff coming from the opposite shore, and saw the Waterman steamer—there was a very strong eddy below the pier I was standing on—I saw the skiff in the eddy, it turned her round—I am speaking of the skiff that was
afterwards upset—it was the only one that was there at the time, I believe—the eddy threw the skiff's head out—I saw the accident happen—I did not hear any orders given on board the steamer, and did not see anything done by the persons on board.
COURT. Q. Did you see whether the steamer changed her course or not? A. I did not—I saw the skiff, as she came round the stern of the steamer, which was more than 300 yards from where I stood—I did not notice whether the steamer changed her course or not.
JURY. Q. Did you keep your eye on the steam-vessel all the time? A. I saw the boat, as I was walking backwards and forwards—I did not lose sight of the steamer at all—if she had altered her course materially I should have seen it—a little deviation, perhaps, I might not have noticed—I kept my eye on her—I know she did not deviate twenty feet from her course, but if she did five or ten it might not have been noticed by me—she did not change her course anything to speak of—I observed that she did not change her course.
SIR JOHN BAYLEY . Q. Did you notice that her paddles were stopped? A. Yes, just previous to the accident—half a second before—it was a momentary thing, just previous to her striking, just after she had cleared the stern of the schooner—I did not hear anybody hail on board, or any orders given—after the skiff was struck, ropes, and everything that was available, were thrown from the steamer, and every assistance rendered.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did the steamer stop and try to pick the men up? A. The men were floated out into the stream—the steamer did stop for ten minutes—I cannot say it was stationary for ten minutes, but the engine was stopped—I did not see one of the persons that were upset endeavouring to reach the steam-boat—he was outside—they threw over part of the sails and ropes from the steamer—they had no boat—I know nothing more of the steamer than being a passenger in her frequently—not in that particular boat, but in any of the steam-boats—I saw the skiff turned round by the eddy, just when the steam-boat was turning round the stern of the schooner.
COURT. Q. How came the steamer to stop for ten minutes, what was she doing during that time? A. She was coming to the pier for passengers, and she stopped on the accident taking place—I was waiting for her—she just glided to the pier—I got on board—she did not go on immediately with her passengers—I cannot say as to the time, as there was confusion—I should say it was about ten minutes—I observed that all the persons were thrown out of the skiff—I cannot say how many there were—they were picked up before the steamer went off—all that could be found at the time—I do not know how many were picked up—I was told they were all picked up—I was on board the steamer—we went on understanding all were picked up—that was my impression at the time.
RICHARD WHEELER . I was master of the Orana steam-boat, running to Ipswich—I was a passenger on board the Waterman No. 6 steamer, on Friday morning, the 25th Oct.—I saw a schooner lying aground by the Lower Tunnel pier—I saw a skiff after we passed the schooner—I did not see it before because my attention was not called to it—I cannot exactly say what distance she was from the steam-boat as we passed the schooner, it was so momentary—she was not above her own length and a half from the steam-vessel—my attention was called to her by the master calling out to ease the vessel, as soon as we got abreast of the stern of the
schooner—that order was instantly obeyed—the schooner occasioned a very great eddy—the skiff was in the eddy, and seemed unmanageable altogether with it—if I had been in command of the Waterman myself, in my judgment I could not have avoided the accident, nor could any other man on the river.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. If you had been in command, and on the paddle box of the vessel, should you have kept a good look out? A. Yes—I think I should be able to see a boat if it was crossing the river, if it was about 150 feet before me—it would be my duty to keep at the same speed as before, if the boat was across my bows—would not run right into the boat of course—I could prevent that by easing and stopping the vessel—I should consider it my duty to do so.
Q. If you were in command should you think it your duty to go at the rate of ten miles an hour round a place without knowing what was behind it? A. Yes—I should not think it necessary to lessen the speed, although I could not see what was behind the place we were going round.
JURY. Q. Will you stake your knowledge of your business, and your own character, that you would not stop your vessel in going round such a place? A. Very often in places we are obliged to go in that way, to get the vessel out of a little bother.
COURT. Q. Was there a little bother on this occasion? A. Well, I did not observe that—very often we have to shove ahead at the rate of ten miles an hour to prevent an accident—I did not observe whether there was any such occasion here—I am not aware that it would have been proper on this particular occasion to have gone slower round the Point.
MR. BALLANTINE. Were you on the look-out? A. No, I was only a passenger; it was, of course, the business of the man on the paddle-box to look out and see whether there was a boat ahead—very likely he had an opportunity of seeing the boat before I did; of course he had, because he was higher than I was—he would have an opportunity of seeing it as soon as we cleared the end of the schooner—that might have been two seconds before I saw her—at the time he called out, "Ease her," I looked ahead and saw the danger—we were just past the schooner's stern when he called out, "Ease her"—he could not see her before that, that I am aware of—he saw her as soon as he could—she was a boat's length and a half from the vessel when I saw her—I saw her the moment he called out, and I believe he called out the moment he could see her—I do not know when he saw her—he saw her before I saw her, at least he called out to ease the vessel, I could not tell when he could see her; I was not observing, and did not see the boat till he called out to ease her—the thing that prevented him from seeing was the stern of the schooner—we were in the act of passing the stern of the schooner when he called out, "Ease her"—I do not know whether that was the first time he could see her—his attention might have been called to her—I did not see her till he called, "Ease her."
COURT. Q. The schooner hindered him from seeing the skiff for some time? A. Yes—it ceased to hinder him at some time—I cannot say how soon it was after it ceased to hinder him from seeing the boat that he called out—we were just passing the end of the schooner as he called out, "Ease her."
SIR J. BAYLEY. Q. If you had seen the skiff on your larboardbow, it would have been your duty to have eased her? A. Of course—if
she was across our bows we should not ease her at all—there would not be the same necessity to ease her when she had crossed our bows and got on our starboard bow.
THOMAS JOYCE . I am a leather-dresser. I was a passenger on board the Waterman steamer on this day. I was on the cabin stairs at the time the accident happened—I did not see the blow struck, but I heard and felt it—before I felt the blow the engines were positively stopped—there were several passengers on deck—it was rather a wet morning, and many were down in the cabin.
THOMAS LILLY . I am a waterman. I was a passenger on board the Waterman steamer on the 25th of Oct.—I saw the schooner aground by the Lower Tunnel-pier—I was standing on the starboard side of the paddle-box, just before it—I saw the skiff, I should suppose, about half a minute before we rounded the schooner's stern—as soon as I saw the skiff, I heard the orders, "Ease her," "Stop her," and "Turn her astern," given by the master—those orders were given in succession; I suppose, in half a second, as soon as he could repeat them—the skiff was in the eddy when I saw her—the eddy had a great effect on her—nobody has the power of pulling in a boat when it gets into an eddy—she was quite unmanageable—she was turned round towards the steamer, not exactly round—in my judgment, the eddy, and nothing else, caused the collision—I can give no other account—had no schooner been there, and nothing to cause the eddy, the boat would have gone into the stairs before the steamer found her.
COURT. Q. Were you very much astonished when you saw the eddy? A. No, not at all, because I was certain that the schooner lying aground would cause an eddy—I knew there must be an eddy before I saw it.
RALPH SCOTT . I am a waterman's apprentice. On the morning of the 25th of Oct. I was on the Upper Tunnel-pier, and saw the accident—I did not see anything done by the steamer in order to avoid it—the accident would not have occurred if the schooner had not been where she was—she created an eddy—in my judgment the skiff could not have been seen by the steamer before she rounded the schooner.
COURT. Q. The captain of the steamer would see the schooner there, would he not? A. Yes—the eddy drew the boat's head down, and threw it out into the tide again—if the captain had seen the boat a hundred yards before he came to her, and had stopped the engine, or turned the helm starboard, that would have prevented the accident.
SAMUEL BRISTOW . I am mate on board the Waterman steamer, No. 6. I was on board on the 25th of Oct.—I saw the skiff when I was abreast of the schooner's stern—the first thing I did was to hail the skiff.
COURT. Q. Whereabouts were you? A. I was on the fore starboard deck—the prisoner was on the paddle-box—it was his business to look out—he was the person who was directing the motions of the vessel.
SIR J. BAYLEY. Q. What did you hail the skiff to do? A. To keep clear of us—she would have done so had it not been for the eddy—I cannot say how near she was to us then—I should say within something less than fifty or sixty yards when I first saw her, thereabouts—I cannot say exactly—it might be about two of our vessel's length—I cannot say exactly—I dare say our vessel is ninety feet long—I did not hear any orders given by the captain when I first hailed her—I believe I hailed her first—when I saw her in the eddy coming towards us, I heard the captain say, "Ease
her, stop her"—I believe those orders were obeyed—I ran to pick up a boat-hook for the purpose of defending the boat off if she came nigh us, which she did—I saw the boat fly out into the eddy, and then ran, and picked up the boat-hook—I could not keep her off—I was there in time to do so, but the boat rowing towards us, and coming out in the eddy rapidly, and our boat keeping way at the time, I had no power to keep her off.
Q. Did you hear any orders given about altering your course? A. The word "Starboard" was given—I believe that was before the words "Ease I her, stop her"—I believe the man at the helm put the helm a starboard by the course I saw her come—I was not looking out, I was looking at the boat at the time, but judging from the head of the boat, and the way she went, I judged the helm must have been starboarded, otherwise her head would not have come out from the shore as it did—the boat would have gone under the wheel before, had it not been for the man pulling with one hand and stopping the other—that was a proper direction to give in order to avoid a collision.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long have you been on board the Waterman? A. About a year and nine months.
Q. Supposing you were fifty yards from an object perfectly stationary, and you going at full speed, should you be able to stop your vessel before coming up to that object? A. You may easily stop the engine, but the vessel would not stop in the time—the engine might be stopped—if we were fifty yards from an object, and going with the tide, we could not prevent our steamer from going over that object—if we backed our engines, and starboarded our helm, that would not avoid it with the tide in our favour—we had a man at the helm—if the object, instead of lying still, were making to the right, we should be able to avoid it by starboarding our helm, which would throw our head away—that would, take very little time—the boat came to us faster than our helm would act.
COURT. Q. You say he called out, "Starboard" just before "Ease her, stop her? A. Yes, we were then so close to the boat that by the time I got up the boat-hook she was striking against us, at the time the helm was starboarded the boat was about her own length from us, about twenty-three feet—the object of starboarding the helm was to clear the boat if possible—if that had been done when we were fifty yards off, we might have got further out of her way, her head could have been thrown more off.
Q. Then, if at the time when you hailed the boat, when she was unmanageable, your helm had been starboarded, your vessel would have cleared her? A. The boat was not unmanageable at the time I first saw her—she was rowing down fair in the tide—she was not in the eddy when I first saw her—I do not think we should have got clear of her if our helm had been starboarded at the time I hailed her.
Q. Then you tried to do that at twenty feet which would be impossible in fifty yards? A. If it had not been so we should have struck the boat in the other bow, by backing we threw her head rather more in shore, whereas we should have struck her on the other bow had it not been so—I believe I was the first person that called out—I did not notice that the prisoner called out or took any notice of her—the first I heard him say was, "Starboard the helm"—that was too late, because the eddy had then taken the boat's head—if there had been nothing but the true tide she would have cleared us, had we been going as fast as we could possibly
go—it was the fault of the eddy entirely—I was aware there would be an eddy when I saw the schooner aground.
JAMES KERR . I am a waterman—I was on board the Waterman steamer on the 25th of Oct., and was at the wheel—after we pasted the schooner, which was aground alongside Lower Tunnel-pier, the captain gave me orders to put the helm a-starboard—I was in the act of doing so at the time we passed the schooner—it was before we passed the schooner that he gave me the order—I knocked the helm hard a-starboard with the stool.
COURT. Q. Was that in order to pass the schooner? A. Yes—it was the usual order at that place, in order to pass the pier-head to go in to the stairs.
SIR J. BAYLEY. Q. In your way up from Woolwich had you called at Greenwich? A. Yes, we kept the Surrey shore part of the way up from Greenwich, till we bad directions to go to Limehouse-pier at Limehouse-hole—we came out from Limehouse, and made a direction as clear at we could come up the river as far as the Tunnel-pier—I remember the collision taking place.
Q. Did you receive any orders what to do with the helm previous to that? A. After the weigh of the boat was off, I had orders from the captain, and I put the helm according to which was the right way for it to be put, in order to put her into the Tunnel-pier—after this wherry was stopped I shifted the helm—I did not feel the knock against the wherry—I did not see the collision, and did not feel it exactly—I heard it mentioned that we had run a boat down—no order was given me just before that, further than to put the helm hard a-starboard—that was an order given to get out of the way of the boat, and, as usual, to get into the pier.
COURT. Q. Then it so happened that at the time you wanted to get out of the way of the boat, you wanted to get in to the pier? A. Yes, that is the usual course when we are going to the Upper Tunnel-pier—we go in towards the Lower Tunnel-pier, and put the helm hard a-starboard, and shoot in alongside the Upper Tunnel-pier—after the order was given, I blocked the helm hard a-starboard, and ran to the starboard quarter—I threw a stern rope to the assistance of the persons that were overboard, and I lent a gentleman a hand to get a stool to chuck overboard, to restore the people's lives.
GEORGE THORNTON . I was the engineer on board the Waterman No. 6, on the 25th of Oct., when this accident happened. I had been on deck previous to arriving at Lower Tunnel-pier—I went below, and in quite two minutes after I got below I received the orders, "Ease her, stop her, turn her astern"—as well as I can judge, we were about the Lower Tunnel-pier at that time—those orders were given rapidly, almost as quick as they could be given—I obeyed those orders.
WILLIAM SALVAGE . I am a clerk in Somerset-house. I was a passenger on board the Waterman steamer on the 25th of Oct.—I saw a schooner lying athwart the tide, between the two Tannel piers—shortly after, I heard the captain cry out in one breath, "Ease her, stop her, turn her astern"—I got on to the gangway, and saw a skiff, with five or six men in her—she shot out, and came in contact with the fore part of the right hand paddle-box of the steamer—the boat was upset, and the persons all immersed in the water—in my judgment the accident decidedly could not have been avoided.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How old are you? A. Between seventeen and eighteen—I have had very little experience in steam navigation, or the management of steam-boats—I have never been captain, mate, or engineer—I have very little experience.
COURT. Q. What is your line? A. Clerk in the Registry-office—I have been there six months—I was with my father before that—I had just left school.
SIR J, BAYLEY . Q. Do you live at Plumstead, in Kent? A. Yea—that is about a mile and a half from the river—I have been in the habit of going up and down every day, for the last six months.
(Captain Ralph, formerly in the Hon. East India Company's service, and Sir Jamei Gordon, Lieutenant Governor of Greenwich Hospital, gave the prisoner a good character as a careful, steady, and skilful man.)
GUILTY.— Strongly and earnestly recommended to mercy on account of his previous good character. — Confined Four Months .
Before Mr. Justice Maule.
[trialAccount t18441125-182]
182. HENRY BRENTON was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Mary Tremlett Turner Brenton. He was also charged on the Coroner's Inquisition with the like manslaughter.
MR. HAKE conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY WILLIS LAYER , I am a surgeon, and live at 38, Stamford-street, Lambeth. I knew Mrs. Brenton, the deceased—I was called to attend her about the 17th of Aug., and I attended her in the delivery of female child on the 20th of Aug.—she left her bed after that at about the ordinary period—she had a very good time—it was quite a natural labour—I believe it was not her first child, but I never attended her before—after leaving her bed she was attacked with rheumatism—I think she left her bed about the third week, or probably rather before—I do not know how long after leaving her bed she returned to it—I think several days, but I was not there all the time—I merely called in occasionally to see how she was, after being attacked with rheumatism—my attendance then recommenced—she was then keeping her bed—I left town on the 25th of Sept. I think, and left the case in charge of Mr. Saunders—I returned on the 10th of Oct., and on that evening I saw the deceased with her father, whom I was then introduced to, and Mr. Saunders—she was then in a state of almost insensibility—she was paralyzed in the right arm, and the left leg appeared paralyzed—she had spasms of the muscles of the jaw, producing what is commonly called locked-jaw—she had a very low feeble pulse—she could only be aroused to answer a question or two, not a sequence of questions—I was shown a wound which was then sloughing on her back—it was about two inches long and one wide—it was a slough in the centre with a red margin, and shading off to the natural colour of the skin—it was on the sacrum, over the lower bone of the back, not quite the lowest—Mr. Brenton returned with roe to my house for medicine, and he said, seeing I was returned, and being his wife's medical attendant, he thought it right to state to me what had occurred on that particular evening in question, that he was desirous of having intercourse with his wife, which she resisted, and thinking it arose from ill temper, or words to that effect, he had persisted, and was exceedingly sorry for the results—I think he remarked that he thought it rather extraordinary the bruise on the back
had not been discovered by the medical men on their first attendance after the 3rd—I am not certain whether it was at that period, or on any future occasion, that that occurred—Mrs. Brenton died on the 19th of Oct.—I was not present at her death—she was dead when I called that day—I made a post mortem examination on the morning of the inquest, the Monday following, and the day previous—it occupied many hours—we took a portion one day, and a portion the next—Mr. Saunders was present with me on the first day, and Mr. Fisher, Mr. Saunders, and myself on the second day, the day of the inquest—the father of the deceased was not present—I believe he was in the house a portion of the time, but not in the room—( referring to his minutes)—I first opened' the skull—on removing the skull, I saw nothing of any importance on the outer surface of the brain—there was an effusion of about two ounces and a half of serum in the arachnoid sac, which is one of the cavities of the brain—that was more than the ordinary quantity—there were no external marks of injury on the head—the vessels of the brain were in a turgid state, more particularly on the left side, where there was a slight degree of adhesion of the membranes of the brain—there was about two drachms of serum in the ventricle—that was not material—that was all I saw the first day—on the second day's examination, I saw external marks of sloughing over the sacrum, which I have spoken of as having seen when she was alive—the vessels of the spinal cord, or the marrow, were congested, having patches of red on the top—there were coagula of blood at different portions of the spine, that extended, in different places, from the last dorsal to the cervical vertebree, that is, from the bone of the back to the bone of the neck—then were two dark marks under each knee—the spinal marrow was in a state of inflammation—the symptoms I have mentioned would indicate that inflammation, mation, and others, which showed themselves previously to death, tenderness ness progressing up the spine, and pain, evidenced by flinching on pressure, up the spine externally—I first discovered that tenderness at the sacrum, and some portion of the os nominata, the bones which form the inside of the hip, or the smaller bones of the pelvis—it extended then up to the first dorsal bone of the back—on further examination I found that it extended further up the dorsal—the sacrum bone was exceedingly tender, so that she could not bear pressure on it, and flinched whenever it was touched—inflammation was the cause of that tenderness—the bone was also enlarged—I attribute that, also, to the inflammation—after death I found that the surface of the bone, immediately under the slough, was dark, as if it had lost its vitality, as if it had been mortified during life—there was a degree of cares, or softening of the bone immediately under it—that state of the bone was decidedly connected with the wound that I had observed externally—they acknowledged the same cause—I attribute the death to inflammation, or irritation of the spinal cord—I presume when it extended as far as the origin of the phrenic nerve, the nerve by which we breathe that that was the immediate cause of death—there had evidently been pressure on the spinal marrow from the coagula which was formed there—when the phrenic nerve is affected, life will very soon become extinct—if the phrenic nerve was paralyzed it would cease to act on the diaphragm, and then the diaphragm losing its power, instant death would happen—I suppose that was the cause of death.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. Had you attended this poor lady long previous to her death? A. From about the 17th of August—that
was the first time I had ever seen her—I think she was of a delicate constitution—on my return to town she was labouring under a shock of the nervous system—the system generally had received a shock—I do not think such a state would exist if a person died from an attack of apoplexy—I should say locked jaw is certainly not very often produced by an attack of apoplexy—apoplexy may be accompanied by paralysis of the arm—apoplexy produces a depression, a loss of power of the nervous system—I think I never attended a person who died from concussion of the brain.
Q. Could you tell what would be the difference between a person dying from apoplexy and from concussion of the brain? A. I think, if I attended from an early period, I could, no doubt, form an opinion—the appearances of the deceased's head did not indicate sufficient symptoms of apoplexy—the great mischief here was in the spine, and my previous knowledge of the case made me suppose so the more—I saw nothing in the head to produce apoplexy—when I observed the wound on the spine I did not attribute it to the effect of disease—not at any time.
ELIZABETH LEE . I am a nurse—I attended Mrs. Brenton from the 19th of Aug. up to the time of her death—I remember the night of the 3rd of Oct.—I left Mrs. Brenton at eleven o'clock that night—I left Mr. Brenton in the bedroom with her—I went into the front parlour—they were in the back parlour—I slept in the front parlour that night—after I had been there ten minutes I heard Mrs. Brenton say, "You may pinch me, or take pieces out of me, but I will die before I will give in to you"—I heard a scuffling first of all, and that expression directly, at the tame time—the scuffling continued about an hour, from eleven till twelve—I did not hear Mr. Brenton say anything—I heard no other remark from Mrs. Brenton in that hour—they were quiet for a little while, and then I heard them begin again—that was about a quarter after twelve—I heard her call him, "You wretch," and "a beast"—she said, if she had had Cobley, he would not hare used her in that way—I heard nothing else—that was the last I heard—I heard no sounds or any groaning, that I swear—I have never stated so—the baby, six weeks old, was in the room—it was crying in the course of the night, two or three times, and I heard Mr. Brenton speak to it, and hushing it—he was crying "Hush"—about half-past six or seven in the morning I knocked at their door—Mr. Brenton told me to come in—I leant over and took the baby out of the bed—Mr. Brenton was in bed at the time—I saw Mrs. Brenton—I asked her what was the matter, but received no answer—she was in an insensible state, but I had seen her so several times—after this I left the room, and went into the front room with the, child—I prepared the breakfast at eight o'clock, and took it in—Mrs. Brenton did not get up to eat any—she was not well enough to take any—Mr. Brenton got up about a quarter to eight, and went into the front parlour, and took breakfast at the same table with me—I did not put any question to him about Mrs. Brenton—I did not say a word to him about what I had heard—I am married—I attended Mrs. Brenton all this time—she was in a very weak, debilitated state, and had been so during the eighteen months I had attended her—Mr. Brenton went out about nine that morning—I told Mrs. Baker, the landlady, what had taken place, as soon as he had gone out—it might be twenty minutes after he was gone, I cannot say exactly—Mrs. Baker did not send for a medical man on seeing her—she did not do anything—we had a medical man, who always came, every day, about twelve o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What was the nature of this scuffling, in your opinion? A. I think he wished to cohabit with his wife—that appeared to be his endeavour—she never complained of any blow being struck—there was no cry out, nor anything that could lead me to believe any blow had been struck—she seemed to be struggling, and not to be inclined to allow him—she was an obstinate temper, and I could tell she refused his purpose, and he was endeavouring to effect it—this was six weeks after her confinement—I attended her once before when she miscarried—the prisoner is twenty-two years of age—she was always ailing ever since I have known her—he has been a most excellent husband to her as ever lived—I do not think a better man ever lived—I know what a✗ good husband is—I have got a very bad one myself—I go out at a✗ monthly nurse—the prisoner always behaved with kindness to hit wife, and waited on her—at one period he was in very comfortable circumstances—after his marriage his circumstances somewhat declined—when I first went there, till the last two months, during her confinement, she had two feather-beds on her bed, six pillows, two bolsters, and about seves blankets, not on the bed, but under it, and when she was confined she had a hard flock bed and flock mattress, which, she said, frequently made her bones sore—the things were pledged, as they were not so well off—it was in consequence of their reduced circumstances—they had been pledged to procure necessaries for himself and wife—for her comforts, of course, as well as his own—he was too fond of his wife, I am sorry to say, a woman that drank so—a Dr. Fisher came to see her—at the time he came she was not sensible—she was never sensible, during the sixteen days, from the morning it happened till she died—I was with her when she died—her husband always behaved kindly to her, as kind as a man could be—at the time she miscarried she was in a state of insensibility very much resembling this state, and the landlady, who is outside, can say the same.
MR. HAKE. Q. Have you seen Mr. Brenton since he has been in custody? A. No, only at Union-hall—I had no conversation with him there—Mr. Brenton was an excellent husband to his wife ever since I knew them—I recollect Mrs. Brenton having a black eye, but it wai not given her by Mr. Brenton, it was by being intoxicated, and that very day I fetched her seven quarterns of gin, and then, before Mr. Brenton came home, I fetched a jug of hot water to bring it up again—she got the black eye by falling down the stairs—he never struck her in his life in my presence—on the 3rd of Oct. Mrs. Brenton had made no complaint to me of any sore on the lower part of her back.
COURT. Q. How long did you remain nursing her on this last occasion? sion? A. Eight weeks—the whole time from her delivery to her death—I slept in the front parlour—Mr. Brenton was away during six weeks—the first month he was away—he came home for a day or two, and then went away for another fortnight.
JURY. Q. Was the mattress on the floor, or on a bedstead? A. On a French bedstead—there were laths underneath—it was a very thin mattress—the flock bed was taken away by order of the doctors, and shelaid on the bare mattress—the doctor said it would be better.
COURT. Q. He went away for a month, beginning from the time of her delivery? A. Yes—he was gone three or four days, or a week before her delivery—I cannot exactly say the day he went—he returned in a month and two days—he then slept with his wife—she had been up once
or twice in that time, but not more—he then went away again, and returned in a fortnight—he then slept with Mrs. Brenton—this happened within a day or two after the six weeks—the first night he came home after the month, she wished me to go home and sleep, because Mr. Brenton was coming to sleep with her—I did not sleep in the parlour that time—there are very thin folding-doors between the parlours—she was always ailing—at the time Mr. Brenton came home at the end of the month I asked her whether she was in a fit state for him to sleep with her, and she said the was as well as she ever was in her life—I went to her mother's to sleep for three nights before this happened, as she wished me—the first and only night I slept in the front parlour, when Mr. Brenton was sleeping in the back parlour with his wife, was the night this happened—he had been home some nights before, sleeping with her, and I was away from the parlour lour on those nights, because Mrs. Brenton desired me—she told me her husband was coming to sleep with her, as she had said before—I did not speak to her about her weak state then—I thought there was no, occasion—she got the black eye by tumbling down when intoxicated—she got so very often during the whole time I knew her, except when she was confined—I do not suppose she ever went a day without having six or seven quarterns unknown to her husband, end when I would not fetch it, she would send her little girl, nine years old, and when she was not at home she would send the potboy—that was not while she was at Mrs. Baker's—she had not been at Mrs. Baker's above three weeks before she was confined—she did not get drunk while she was at Mrs. Baker's—she left it off when she was confined—there was nothing in the scuffling I heard which induced me to suppose any violence was used—it was nothing more than might take place between husband and wife—I did not like to interpose as I was not called—had I heard any blow given, or anything of that kind, I should have gone in directly, but I did not—the first place at which she was living, where she used to get drunk, was at No. 18, Woodbridge-street, Clerkenwell, and the next was No. 24, Windsor-terrace, City-road.
JOHN HOUSTON . I am a member of the Royal College of Surgeons—I have practised as a surgeon for forty years—I am the father of the deceased—her name was Mary Tremlett Turner Brenton—I think she was thirty-three years old when she died—I cannot speak positively—she was a person of very sober habits—I can speak to that up to the time of her death—I knew her when she was in Woodbridge-street—I visited her. frequently there—I did not visit her in Windsor-terrace—I believe she was there but a very short time—I did not see her there at all—I never saw her intoxicated in my life, from infancy up to the present moment, I do not believe she was ever intoxicated for a single moment—I believe it is a foul calumny, a perjury—I never saw the slightest thing of the kind, nor was she ever the worse for liquor, though I visited her constantly, nor did I ever know of the charge till I heard it brought so vilely in this Court—I never saw her at all the worse for liquor in the whole course of her life—I saw her about ten days before the 3rd of Oct.—she was in a very emaciated and debilitated state, so much so that I was obliged to assist her in getting up in the bed—on Friday, the 4th of Oct., I received a message stating that my daughter was dying, and that she had been supposed to have had some poison given her—I examined her on that occasion—I found there was concussion of the brain, paralysis of the right arm, and a locked jaw—I saw Mr. Saunders, the surgeon,
in the evening—I was there at eight o'clock—I understood he was coming to see her that night—she was treated for concussion of the brain that night—this was on Friday—nothing particular occurred until the following Monday but a progress of the diseased action—I saw her twice every day during the whole of her illness—on Monday I found that there was a morbid action of the bladder and rectum, a paralysis of the two muscles which contain the matter of those parts; and on seeing her endeavouring to move her leg, I found there was a particular contraction of the left leg, so that she could not turn it over—it was a morbid action—I could not account on the first day for the locked jaw, because that arises from a different cause to concussion of the brain; but when I saw that same action of the leg, I then concluded that there was something the matter with the back—I mentioned that, and I heard the prisoner, on hearing me do so, desire the nurse to tell me that she had a sloughing in the back—I then had her turned over, and examined the back, and there I saw a morbid action about the size of a crown-piece, or not so large probably, perhaps about as large as half-a-crown—there was a vesicle, and the morbid action of sloughing was going on—from the appearance of the parts, and knowing that the locked jaw, in all probability, arose from some injury of a remote nerve, I concluded at once that a blow had been inflicted on that part, the left leg and thigh being in the same morbid state as the locked jaw; and I beg to observe, that a paralysis of the bladder and rectum always accompanies an injury to the spinal marrow at that part of the back—it was on the lower part of the back, on the sacrum —that is a part of the lumbar region—a concussion cannot take place without a blow—I have seen many concussions of the spine, but I never saw one without a blow—the very term itself signifies that—it is a shock—a concussion necessarily implies external force.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long was your daughter living in Woodbridge-street? A. I think for three months—I think I was there seven times during that period—I did not see her at all at Windsor-terrace—I saw her at the house where she died once, ten days before the 3rd of Oct., but I saw her several times while she was there, because she called upon me frequently, and I had opportunities of seeing her—fifty times—I always gave her money whenever she applied for it—I think three weeks before her confinement she called on me, and I gave her 5s—I attended at the inquest—I was not turned out of the room by order of the Coroner—I was requested to go out of the room, in consequence of being under very excited feelings—I was in the room where they were dissecting my child, and I was not in a sound state of mind—I could not bear my own existence, and I was requested to go out by the Coroner—I beg to say that the bed was ordered to be removed, in consequence of its being in such a filthy state, from the contents of the bladder, that she could not lie on it, and from no other motive.
THOMAS JONES SAUNDERS . I attended Mrs. Brenton, in place of Mr. Laver, when he was out of town—I saw her on the 3rd of Oct.—she was better on that day, and I recommended her to leave her bed—I do not recollect whether she expressed any intention of doing so—she made no complaint to me on that day of any bruise or wound on her person—I called professionally, about one o'clock, on the 4th of Oct.—she was then lying in bed, in a state of insensibility—I had been partly informed by the landlady that there was a probability of her having taken laudanum—Mr.
Brenton was not at home, and the nurse was not there—I had not sufficient information, in a medical way, as to what had occurred to bring Mrs. Brenton into that state, for me to act upon—I called again about seven or eight o'clock in the evening—I then met Mr. Houston—we did not examine her body then—she was very pale, in a state of collapse, the pulse rather small, and the pupils of the eyes not acting—there were symptoms of some shock to the nervous system, and upon that I acted—I saw her again early next morning (Saturday)—I saw her on the Monday—the wound was not pointed out to me till the Tuesday—it was a dark, discoloured surface of skin, with a vesicle or blister upon it, but not a decided wound—I attended the case for seven or eight days altogether—I saw her occasionally after Mr. Laver's return, but not regularly—the wound was on the sacrum —there was tenderness of the sacrum, the result of inflammation, and a redness from the wound—it appeared a wound of recent origin—I should have thought it had existed two, three, or four days, possibly, previous to the Tuesday, but it is uncertain to fix a time—the inflammation gradually extended up the spine—I was present at the post mortem examination—the spine was then opened—we found symptoms of previous inflammation, and there was blood extravasated on the dorsal vertebree more than elsewhere, in fact, there almost alone, principally there, and upwards towards the neck—there was congestion—the sacrum bone was rather soft—I attributed that softness to the inflammation consequent on the external sore—the inflammation of the spine might have arisen from a blow to that part, or, in fact, to any part of the spine, or its course—Mrs. Brenton was a woman of very feeble constitution, which would decidedly make a blow take the greater effect.
Q. Now, looking to the state in which the spinal marrow was, and looking at the whole case before and after death, are yoa of opinion that a blow had been struck on the sacrum? A. I am of opinion that there was a certain shock to the nervous system—how produced I am not fully prepared to say—looking at the state of Mrs. Brenton's health on the 3rd of Oct., I think some severe struggle or conflict might produce those appearances—it must be a shock, a very severe injury to the head or spine—it must be a very severe conflict—striking and beating would concuss the spine, in her weak state especially.
COURT. Q. Might an accidental fall, or knocking against anything hard have produced the effects? A. That might have done it.
MR. HAKE. Q. She had never complained to you of any fall, had she? A. She had not—there were blue marks under the ham of the thigh, as if of bruises.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Small marks? A. Yes, they appeared the result of pinches—they were under the thigh, towards the knee—I saw the bed she was lying on, but did not notice it—having connection in her debilitated state might produce hysteria —it might have some effect on the nervous system—I do not say that the death resulted from the bruise on the sacrum —I do not know from what, but a general shock to the nervous system—I cannot form any opinion that it resulted from the bruise—I saw the body examined—there was no other bruise that could have caused the death—if it was occasioned by anything but the bruise, it must have been by some general nervous derangement—I do not know how to account for it—there might have been some shock to the system which did not leave any external trace—I cannot tell what
it might have resulted from—I have a doubt at this moment—I cannot speak positively.
MR. HAKE. Q. Was there any appearance of hysteria on the 4th, when you saw Mrs. Brenton? A. At first, perhaps, one might feel inclined to think so; but in a very short time my opinion was formed that it was not hysteria, decidedly not, from what I saw subsequently.
COURT. Q. Did you examine the viscera of the stomach? I did, it was healthy—she was a person of very weak constitution—she was of a rheumatic habit, and of a delicate habit altogether—the liver appeared to. lerably healthy—there was nothing about it to excite attention—there was nothing at all in her appearance to indicate a person in the habit of drinking—I ing—I did not notice it—I examined her principally with a view to ascertain the cause of death with relation to the wound, and the stomach.
ELIZABETH BAKER . The deceased and the prisoner were my lodgerssaw Mrs. Brenton on the 3rd of Oct., and on the 4th—she mentioned to me her intention of getting up the next morning—she appeared in better health and spirits than she had for some time—she did not complain of auy sore bones, or any sore upon her person—on the Saturday I was changing the linen with the nurse—Mrs. Houston, the deceased's mother, was there at the time (she often came to see her), and she pointed our attention to the bruise or wound—that was the first time it had been heard of—it appeared to me like a blow—Mrs. Brenton was not at all given to drink, as far as I know—I saw her every day—on the Thursday previous to her death she was sensible—she called me three times by my name, and asked me for some tea—I made her some—she said, "Oh, my back"—I said, "Is your back bad, dear?"—she said, "Yes, Mrs. Baker, it is; Henry kicked me"—she asked me to call for Mrs. Fisher, and she said, "O Mrs. Baker, take care of my baby"—I saw her that day when Mr. Baker was present.
THOMAS FISHER . I am a surgeon. I attended this case the greater part of the time from the 7th Oct., and was present at the post mortem examination the last day—I first saw the bruise on Mrs. Brenton's back on the second day of my seeing her, on the 8th—in my opinion it might have existed about three or four days, or it might have existed longer—I am of opinion that it was caused by violence—I have seen many wounds—I form that opinion from the symptoms combined, from the appearances previous to death and afterwards—I recollect the Saturday on which she died—she stated to me on that day that she believed she would not recover, I was questioning her—I wished to ascertain the state of her mind—her father was present—we were to meet with Mr. Laver, who did not keep his appointment—I was alone with her part of the time, and her father was with me a portion of the time—at the time she made a statement to me I was alone with her—I wished to ascertain her state with respect to her sanity, and to know whether she was sensible enough to answer my questions, to ascertain in what state her health was—I asked her questions about her state of health—I asked her where she was in pain—she said, "In my back"—her father put the next question.
COURT. Q. Tell us what took place when you were alone with her. A. I asked her no more questions then when I was alone with her—after that I was again alone with her, and I asked her if she thought she should recover—she shook her head and began to cry, and said, "No"—I had asked her several questions before that—I asked where
she was in pain—she "In my back"—her father immediately came✗ in and said, "Who did it?"—nothing more took place when I was alone✗ with her the second time—nothing was said after I asked her if she thought she should recover—nothing else was said when I was alone with her, only with her father—something else was said before she shook her head and said "No," but after that, nothing was said either to me alone, or to myself and her father—I never heard her speak after that.
Q. How long was that saying "No" after anything that she said in her father's presence? A. Not a minute—he asked her something, to which she replied—he then went out, leaving her alone with me, and then I asked her if she thought she should recover—that is a very usual question for me to put to my patients, particularly when we find them in a state of insensibility, or collapse—we wish to know the state of the mind, whether they are sensible to answer any questions—I asked her that question to ascertain the state of her mind, and when she said "No" and cried, that satisfied me of the state of her mind, and I asked her no more questions—it is my common practice to put that question to persons in a weak state—I do not know whether it is of service to the patients, or whether it tends to keep up their spirits—I asked her that question to ascertain the state of her mind, to know whether she was sensible or not, in answering the questions I had put to her before with regard to the state of her health.
MR. HAKE. Q. You saw she was dying at that time, I suppose? A. No, I did not—my opinion was that she would not recover.
COURT. Q. Did you ask her whether she had slept well, about her pain, and several other questions? A. Yes—she answered some of those questions reasonably, and some she did not—I asked her where she was in pain three times over—the answer that she gave me once was, "I don't know," and the next time, "In my back"—she answered my questions so that I was in doubt as to the state of her mind—I had some doubts as to whether she was in a sound state of mind or not, and I have some doubts at the present time—I asked the last question to ascertain whether she was in a sound state of mind—she answered, "No," and cried—that answer still left me in doubt, and I cannot now tell whether she was in a sound state of mind or not—I do not know that that question more than any other had a tendency to show whether she was of sound mind or not—I did not think it an unkind or cruel question to put—perhaps it might have been, upon second consideration—I commonly speak cheerfully to my patients, but it is a very frequent question, "Well, do you think you shall recover?"—it depends upon how you express it—I do not think it shows that you think them in danger—I do not know how long this was before she died—Mr. Baker was not present at the time—I think she did expect to die, but I doubt whether she was in a sound state of mind.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
