[trialAccount t18441125-184]
184. ALFRED PRATT was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of Oct., 1 bag, value 1 d.; and 5 sovereigns; the property of Samuel Groves, from his person.
SAMUEL GROVES On the 22nd of Oct., I had five sovereigns in my bag, in my right-hand pocket, at twelve o'clock in the day—it was a life. annuity I had received—I was then in a lane near the Two Brewers, at Clap. ham—I got to that public-house just before twelve—I staid there some time, and then went to a beer-shop—I came out about nine in the evening; and when I got half-way home, in Manor-lane, the prisoner came rustling up against me, and put his hand up my thigh—he put his hand in my pocket, which had no button, and thrust me down—he got my bag—I saw him with it—he put the money into his pocket—he shook the bag in my face, and wanted me to take it—I took it, and found there were only two sovereigns out of five—I said I would be one with him, 1 would have him taken up—I gave the bag up to the policeman, and the two sovereigns—I can swear I had five sovereigns in that bag when he came up to me—I had not taken them out, or changed one of them—I had twelve or fourteen in my pocket beside.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. What may you be? A. A bricklayer's labourer—I know the prisoner—we had been drinking together at the Two Brewers—I had received the five sovereigns on the 14th—I never saw them after twelve o'clock on the 22nd—the first public-house I went into was the Two Brewers, and the prisoner met me there, as I was going in—we staid there an hour, or it might be longer—we had two or three pots, perhaps—we then went to a beer-shop, about three o'clock, and remained till nine—we were there six hours—I paid for seven pots of ale, but several more came in—one had a glass, and another had a glass—we did not drink it ourselves—we smoked our pipes—I left first, and the prisoner came running after me—I could walk as well as 1 can now—the prisoner did not lead me—I did not want leading—I did not rest my arm on his—I never asked him to see me home, and say, after some time, "Let me try if I can't go alone," or put my hands into my pocket, and then reel down—he thrust me down—we only had one glass of gin apiece at the Two Brewers—I said, before the Magistrate, that the prisoner shook the bag in my face, and said, "Here is your money, Sam"—when I had fallen down, the prisoner endeavoured to help me up—after this occurred, the prisoner walked away from me—after I said he had robbed me, we walked about twenty yards together—Mrs. Edwards, the witness, knows me perfectly well—I went home—I did not call a policeman—I could not find one—this was in Manor-lane, not 100 yards from the beer-shop, about 400 yards from Nelson-row, where I live—I slept that night where I lodge, which is at a beer-shop.
SARAH EDWARDS . I am the wife of William Edwards, a coal-dealer, in Little Manor-lane—at half-past nine o'clock in the evening, on the 22nd of October, I heard a person fall under my window—I looked out and saw Groves on the ground, and the prisoner's hand in his pocket—Groves said, "You have robbed me of my money"—the prisoner said he had not—I think the prisoner staid with him five or ten minutes, and then he dragged him across the road—I did not see any more of him.
Cross-examined. Q. Groves was very far gone, was he not? A. Not so far but he knew what he was about—he did not wish to be dragged, but
he could not get away—this is ray mark to this deposition—( read —"The said Sarah Edwards says, 'Last Tuesday night, at half-past nine, I heard some one fall under my window; on going out I saw Groves on the ground, and the prisoner stooping over him; the prisoner put his hand into Groves's right-hand trowsers-pocket; I saw nothing in his hand when he took it out; he put it into his own pocket; Groves said, 'You have robbed me;' the prisoner said he had not; my husband came out, picked up Groves's hat, and said to the prisoner, 'If you have robbed him, give it him, and don't take advantage of him;' Groves called to my husband to go with him; he said he would not.")
WILLIAM EDWARDS . I merely heard Groves cry out, "You have robbed me"—I ran out, and said, " Sam, what are you doing?"—I picked up his hat, and assisted him up—he said, "This man has robbed me"—I said, "If you have taken anything from him, return it to him"—the pritoner did not make any answer—I followed them a few yards to the bottom of the place, and Groves was continually saying, "Follow me, he his taken my money"—I said, "I shall not follow you, if you have taken anything from him, give it him back."
Cross-examined. Q. Who is Billy Bokins? A. He keeps a beer-shop—Groves lodges there—they were then 400 or 500 yards from Bokins—I did not hear the prisoner say, "I have not robbed you, I have not got your money."
JOHN BRIDGES ( police-constable V 194.) I apprehended the prisoner on the 24th of Nov., in the Clapham-road.
Cross-examined. Q. Did the prisoner come to you? A. No, he was just coming from Nelson-row—the prosecutor had not been to me, but he had given information—the prisoner said he had been to the prosecutor's, but he was not at home—I searched the prisoner at the station—I found on him 2 s. 2 d.—I went to his lodging in Robert-street, Stock well—I found nothing there.
NOT GUILTY .

185. HENRY PLEASANT was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Oct., 11 yards of printed cotton, value 10 s. the goods of William Checker; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM CHECKER . I am a linen-draper, and live at Clapham. I had a piece of printed cotton safe at my door at half-past twelve o'clock on the 23rd of Oct.—I received information from a neighbour, and missed it between twelve and one—this is it—it is mine.
JOHN BRIDGLAND . I am an omnibus conductor. On the 23rd of Oct., at ten minutes before one o'clock, I received information, and ran and caught the prisoner—he had this cotton—I saw him drop it—I said, "This don't belong to you"—he said, "No, I did not take it"—he was about 150 yards from Mr. Checker's—he ran part of the way, and then stood, and I came up to him.
Prisoner. There were two others with me who gave it to me.
HENRY NOLAN ( police-constable L 129.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction, by the name of Henry Jones—( read)—the prisoner is the person then tried.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Transported for Seven Years .

186. JOHN HARVEY was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of Nov., 6lbs. weight of ham, value 3 s., the goods of Henry Garrett; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM HELLEWELL . I am in the service of Mr. Henry Garrett, a cheesemonger—he lives in St. George the Martyr, Southwark. On the 14th of Nov. I placed two half hams inside the window at seven o'clock—I do not remember seeing them after that—I saw the prisoner at ten minutes before ten o'clock, about twenty yards from our window, with one of the half hams in his possession—I walked towards him—he saw me, and placed the half ham down by the side of a gateway—he walked on a short distance—I took up the ham, and was returning with it in my apron—the prisoner passed me, and I gave him into custody.
MARY WILKINSON . I am the wife of Joseph Wilkinson. On that evening I saw the prisoner take the ham out of the window which was open—he laid it down by the gateway, then came a few yards towards me, and turned back, and the young man directly gave him into custody.
JAMBS PERRY ( police-constable M 60.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction, which I got from the clerk of the Peace of Surrey—( read)—"Convicted on the 16th of March, 3rd Vict.; and transported for seven years"—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years .

187. FREDERICK CHILD, WILLIAM ROGERS , and HENRY ROGERS , were indicted for stealing, on the 20th of Nov., 1 trowel, value 3 s., the goods of Peter Dunn.
PETER DUNN . I am a plasterer, and live at Kennington. I was at work outside a building at Stock well on the 20th of Nov.—I had a trowel inside the room—I saw it safe at half-past twelve, when I returned from dinner, and missed it at three o'clock—I saw Child and another lad with him, in the road that day, and soon afterwards I saw William Rogers in the room where the trowel was—this is my trowel that I missed.
JURY. Q. How do you know it? A. When I bought it I took the handle off, and put this handle on, and filed it off myself—I can swear to it.
EDWARD WHITE . I am a labourer. I went to look after the prisoners—I saw Child with the trowel under his arm, and the other two prisoners were with him—I followed them, and Child went and pawned it at a pawnbroker's—I went to him when he came out, and asked him if he had seen the Rogers—he told me no, he had just come from Vauxhall-bridge—I told the pawnbroker to keep the trowel.
GEORGE MARK LINTHORNE . I am a pawnbroker. I produce this trowel, which was pawned by a boy, who I think was Child, but I cannot swear to him—White came and told me something in about a quarter of an hour.
Child. I met these two boys; they told me to go there and I could get a job; I went in, and was coming out, when I met William Rogers with the trowel, and he asked me to pawn it, which I did; I was not aware it was stolen.
William Rogers'Defence. He said he had no need of his trowel; he had no work, and would I go and get it for him, it laid on the floor; I went and got, it and he went and pawned it.
(Child received a good character.)
CHILD — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months .
WILLIAM ROGERS **— GUILTY . Aged 12.— Confined Six Months and Whipped .
HENRY ROGERS — NOT GUILTY .

188. ISAAC HOARE and JOHN FLEMING were indicted for stealing, on the 30th of Oct., two wooden boxes, value 2 d., the goods of Richard Herrington.
ANN HERRINGTON . I am the wife of Richard Herrington. We keep a shop at Wandsworth—we had some toy-boxes in a basket in the shop—I saw them safe between one and two o'clock in the day on the 30th of Oct.—I did not miss them till the officer came and brought these two between eight and nine o'clock at night—they are ours.
JOHN POTTER ( police-constable V 118.) I took these boxes from Hoare—he said he had bought them that day, but he did not say where—Fleming was with him, but did not say anything.
Hoare. I bought them of two boys for 1 d.
HOARE — GUILTY .
FLEMING — NOT GUILTY .

189. ISAAC HOARE and JOHN FLEMING were again indicted for stealing, on the 30th of Oct., 1 pound cake, value 3 d.; and 5 Banbury cakes, 5 d.; the goods of James Plumridge.
JAMES PLUMRIDGE . I am a baker, and live at Wandsworth. On the evening of the 30th of Oct. I missed five or six Banbury cakes and a pound cake—they have not been found.
JOHN HALL . I am pot-boy at the Two Brewers. About half-past eight o'clock at night, on the 30th of Oct., I was going out with beer—I saw Hoare with his hand in the prosecutor's window, and Fleming was with him—Hoare had some cakes and some tarts in his hand—they ran across the road together, and ran down Garret-lane.
ALFRED STACEY . I saw Hoare break the window—it was cracked, and he pushed it with his finger—the prisoners ran round the square, and threw the glass away—they then went back and took the cake and tarts out.
Hoare. It was John Webster broke the glass out, and he gave him lome of the tarts.
Witness. I did not see Webster.
HOARE *— GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Four Months .

190. ROBERT EDWARDS was indicted for stealing 3 jackets, value 30 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, 10 s.; 1 shirt, 3 s.; 1 handkerchief; 2 s. 6 d.; and 10lbs. weight of tobacco, 4 s.; the goods of John Dickenson, in a vessel in a port of entry and discharge.
JOHN DICKENSON . I am apprentice on board the Robert Bruce, which was lying off Rotherhithe. I had three jackets and these other articles safe on Monday, the 11th of Nov., between one and two o'clock—I missed them about six o'clock the same evening—these are them—these are all mine.
BENJAMIN HASELDINE . I am a pawnbroker. I produce one jacket,
which I took in pledge of the prisoner on the 11th of Nov., about seven o'clock in the evening.
JOHN EVANS ( police-constable R 190.) I took the prisoner—I told him he was charged with breaking a box on board the Robert Bruce, and stealing these things—he said, "I own I pawned the jacket, but Reed gave it me"—I apprehended Reed, but we could find no evidence against him, and he was discharged—I found a pair of trousers and a silk handkerchief on the prisoner at the station, which the prosecutor identifies.
Prisoner. You asked me whether I pawned the jacket, and I said I did, and Reed gave it me. I was coming down the wall, and Reed said to me, "Will you pawn this jacket?" I said, "Yes, provided you stand some beer." I took the jacket; the man said he would lend me 3 s. on it. I gave Reed the 3 s. and the ticket; he said, "Never mind the ticket, I shan't get it any more."
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Confined Three Months .
[trialAccount t18441125-191]
191. THOMAS SHILLUM and JOHN KING were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of Nov., 1 bag, value 1 s.; 28lbs. weight of shot, 5 s.; the goods of Joshua Walker and others, their masters.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES RATCLIFF ( City police-constable. No. 375.) On the evening of the 8th of Nov. I was on duty in Ludgate-street, between six and seven o'clock—I saw King carrying something—I followed him to the Old Bailey, and saw he had got something very heavy under his arm—when he got to the Sessions-house, I said, "What have you got here?"—he said, "It is all right, old fellow"—he said a party gave it him, and I might go and look for that party—I said, "No, I shall look to you"—he said, "I shall lose my place; for God's sake let me go"—he made two struggles to get away in the Old Bailey, but at last I succeeded in getting him to the station, and there I found it was this bag of shot which he was carrying, which has the name of "Walker, Parker, and Co." on it—I went to Messrs. Walker, Parker, and Co., and went with the foreman to Shillum, and took him to the station, and then King said that Shillum gave it him, but he asked him for it, and Shillum took it out of the ware-house.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When King was asked how be became possessed of it, did he not say that Shillum gave it to him to carry for him? A. No, he said he asked him for it—I heard Mr. Wardley ask Shillum where he got the bag, and I heard Shillum reply, "I took the bag of shot from the warehouse, and gave it to King."
MR. DOANE. Q. You heard King say, before that, that he got it from Shillum, but he asked him for it? A. Yes.
GEORGE WARDLEY . I am manager of the factory of Joshua Walker and others—King was our carman, and Shillum was in the white lead department—we have a shot department—persons can get from the white lead department to the shot department—Shillum had no business in the shot department without special orders—I remember King going away on the night of the 8th of November, about six o'clock—the policeman came afterwards, and I went with him to Shillum's house—I found him at home, and took him to the station—I left him outside—I went in and found King in custody—I saw this bag, with my employers'name on it—I asked King how he became
possessed of it—he said, "Shillum gave it me to carry a little way for him"—I went outside, and asked Shillum to go in—King then again repeated that Shillum gave him this bag of shot—I asked Shillum how he became possessed of it—he said, "I stole it from the warehouse, and gave it to him to carry a little way, but I was asked to do so"—this bag of shot is worth 5 s.
Cross-examined. Q. How long has King been with you? A. Nearly two years—he has a wife, and I believe one daughter.
Shillum. Q. Did I hesitate to accompany you? A. Not at all—I left you outside the station-house, and found you there—I have entrusted you with notes, money, and checks, and always found you correct before—I did not say if you told the truth I would not cause you to be locked up—you were locked up before that—when you were at Union-hall, King sent for me, and wanted me to be merciful—I said there might be some mercy shewn them if they would tell their confederates, and what had been done previous to this.
MR. DOANE. Q. You found King and Shillum at Union-hall? A. I did not see Shillum—King said, "Master, I hope you won't punish me for this little offence; I hope you will forgive me"—I said, "I can't, it must be left to my employers."
Shillum's Defence. It was from an inducement held out to me by the prosecutor, that I stated what I did; several policemen stated that if I spoke the truth my master would not hurt roe; it was in consequence of that, and King saying that I gave it him, that I stated what I did, but I am innoccent of the transaction as a child.
James Brannan police-sergeant G 20, Joseph Seal, a coal-dealer, North-street, Finsbury, and Richard Phillips, a porter, in Turnraill-street, gave King a good character.
SHILLUM — GUILTY . Aged 32.
KING — GUILTY . Aged 40.
Confined Six Months .
[trialAccount t18441125-192]
192. MARTHA TAYLOR and GEORGE WILSON were indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. DOANE and SCRIVEN conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS PICKETT . I am a chandler, and live at Upper Tooting. On the 16th of Nov., about four or five o'clock, Taylor came to my shop and purchased a small quantity of cheese, which came to fourpence half-penny—she gave me in payment a half-a-crown—I gave her the change—she did not speak, nor did I, but she took the two shillings, and left the three halfpence on the counter, and was going away—I called her back—she came and took the three halfpence, and I saw no more of her—I afterwards saw Mr. Barnett—I gave him the same half-crown, as I got from Taylor.
Wilson. Q. What did you do with the half-crown? A. I had it in my hand till Mr. Barnett came in—he marked it, and gave it me again—he came in not a minute after Taylor left.
MARY WOODS . I am the wife of Joseph Woods, a corn-chandler, at Balham-hill. On the 16th of Nov. Taylor came into my shop for a pint of hird-seed—it was rape and canary—it came to 3 d.—she gave me a half-crown—I gave her 2 s. 3 d., and put the half-crown into my pocket—I am quite certain I had no other half-down in my pocket—Mr. Barnett came in with two constables—he wished to look at the half-crown, and I gave it
him—my shopman marked it in my presence—I had not received any other half-crown from the time Taylor came in.
Wilson. Q. Do you positively swear you did not put it into the till when you took it from the woman? A. Yes, I do.
JOHN BARNETT . I am a dairyman, and live at Lower Tooting, On the 16th of Nov., in the evening, I saw Taylor go into a shop—I went afterwards, and made inquiries—in consequence of what I was told I watched Taylor, and saw her join company with Wilson—they were in company side by side, and talking—I then saw Taylor go into Mr. Pickctt's shop—Wilson was on the opposite side—when Taylor came out she crossed, and joined him—I went into Mr. Pickett's, and received the half-crown which I marked—it was afterwards given to the officer—when I came out I saw the two prisoners again—Wilson was a little in advance of Taylor—she then went into two or three other shops, and when she came oat of them she joined Wilson, and they went on—I followed, and saw Taylor go into Wood's shop, and come out—I crossed over to Wilson, who I thought was watching me, and offered to sell him a little spaniel dog that I had—he said he did not want it—I walked a little way on, and saw he was standing waiting for Taylor—she joined him, and they walked up Balham-hill together—I met my brother-in-law, Passfield, and we looked out for a constable—we went to the Bedford Arms—the prisoners came up and I took Wilson, Passfield took Taylor.
Wilson. Q. Were we together when you first saw us? A. No—you joined company when Taylor came out of a house—you kept on together from Lower to Upper Tooting.
WILLIAM PASSFIELD . I am brother-in-law to Mr. Barnett. On the 16th of Nov. I met him on Balham-hill—I assisted him in watching the prisoners—I saw them together, and heard them in conversation—I could hear them talking, and it seemed to me that they were wrangling—I took Taylor—they were together at the time I took her.
GEORGE SPENCER DAWES ( police-constable V 185.) I took the prisoners at the Bedford Arms—I found on Wilson four shillings, a sixpence, 4 1/2 d. in copper, a small quantity of cheese, and a small quantity of bird-seed, which was rape and canary mixed—I found on Taylor four shillings, two sixpences, and 4 d. in copper—I produce the two half-crowns, the one from Mrs. Wood, and the other which was passed at Mr. Pickett's
MART WOODS re-examined. This is the same sort of seed that I sold to Taylor—I sold her a pint—here is not quite a pint.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These half-crowns are both counterfeit, and cast in the same mould.
TAYLOR — GUILTY . Aged 21— Confined Three Months .
WILSON — GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined One Year .
[trialAccount t18441125-193]
193. HENRY PERFECT and JOHN PERFECT were indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of Oct., 1 pillow, value 2 s.; 3 locks, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 coach-spring, 1 l.; 2 sacks, 3 s.; part of a scythe, 4 d.; 2 knives, 6 d.; 1 punch, 2 d.; 1 pair of tongs, 1 s.; 1 shovel, 6 d.; 1 file, 6 d.; part of a desk-flap, 5 s.; 1 collar, 1 s.; 6 coach-bolts, 1 s.; 1 cupboard-door, 5 s.; 2 blocks for springs, 2 s.; and 1 piece of wood, 6 d.; the goods of Joseph Skinner.
JOSEPH SKINNER . I am an auctioneer and appraiser, and live at No. 33, Park-place, New Peckham. A fortnight before Michaelmas last I took a
cottage, of which Henry Perfect was keeping possession for the landlord—I put in a large quantity of my property about a week before Michaelmas—Henry Perfect was then in the house, and a person whom I believed to be his wife—the articles stated in the indictment were among the things I put in—I have lost a great many things, and have only found a few of them—I have not had anything to do with them since I put them in—I have been in the country—I know nothing of John Perfect, more than his coming two or three times with some potatoes to his brother—he came with a horse and cart—Henry said he hoped I should excuse his taking the liberty of putting his brother's horse and cart in the place—on the 1st of Nov. the things were gone from the house—those that are here, and a number of others.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How long have you had these things? A. The principal part for years—this is part of a spring belonging to a phaeton I had—I found it when I went in pursuit of Henry, who ran sway—I went to his brother's house at Singlewell, thinking he might be there, and there found this spring—it was fixed to a cart of John Perfeet's—I know it quite well, and I know this part of a scythe—it is part of a scythe which came with a lot of things which I was a great loser by, and it was bound up with some tar cord—it had no handle to it then, but it had the same notch in it which it has now—it is in a very different state now—I know this file—it is a very unusual sort, such as I never saw in my life before—I purchased it, and found great fault with it—the point was broken off—this piece of wood is a desk-flap—I bought a desk with four flaps to it, and I have only one left—this has been matched to it, and this part exactly fits where it was taken from—I found this at John Perfect's, in a shed which they call an oil place—my son went and saw John carry sway his own furniture from the cottage, and some other things which my son could not see—I had some complaints and suspicions against Henry which took me to John's place—I took handcuffs with me—I am a parish constable—I have nothing to do as a constable at Gravesend, near to where I took him—I handcuffed him—he has not been very ill in consequence of that—if I had not had assistance I should have lost my life—I hare a witness to show where he struck me on the head—I cannot recollect what he said—if he did not deny that the property was mine, it was to that purpose.
COURT. Q. When you got down to John Perfect's what did you do? A. I had his master with me, who charged him with robbing him of potatoes and straw—I asked for his brother—he said he had just heard, last night, that his brother was in custody, and when he found I was going to take him into custody, and put the handcuffs on him, he struck me over the head with the bit of the scythe.
MR. DOANE. Q. Did he ask if you had any warrant? A. No—he might have said something about a warrant afterwards, when he was at a public-house—I do not know a boot and shoemaker at Paddington—I never did—I do not know a man who had a lodging in South Moulton-lane, Davis-street—there was a man named Hiscock, who rented a shoemaker's stall of me—the account there states he was a little in arrears—I distrained upon him, but not for a little rent—his rent was 4 s. a week, and be owed me 5 l.—I did not take his apron off—I took his coat, but not off his person—I took his tools—his spectacles were taken with the other things, not off his person—I have heard that he hung himself from the effects of
drunkenness—I do not know that the Jury expressed indignation at my conduct—I was not there—I have seen in the paper that they did, and I have been to the office of the Times to-day about it—you will see a different statement to-morrow.
COURT. Q. Are you sure that the things you found at John Perfect's are yours? A. Yes—I have brought another spring to match this one—I know this pillow, and believe it to be mine—this is one of my sacks—I claimed two at John Perfect's—this one was cut open and laid upon the ground—this has the maker's name on it.
SUSANNAH FRANKLIN . I went to the station with Mr. Skinner—I know this pillow and tongs.
THOMAS BENJAMIN BAKER SKINNER . I went to Rose Cottage, and the prisoners were loading a cart—I could not discover what things were in it—I did not stop—John Perfect drove it—when I went back the things at the bottom were all covered over, and then they put their own things on the top—they had got some of their own things there.
Cross-examined. Q. You were present during the whole time they loaded the cart? A. No—I went after they had got a good many things in the bottom of the cart—I then went home and told my mother, and then went back—John Perfect saw me—I do not know whether the things he carried away were his own or not.
Henry Perfect's Defence. I could take my solemn oath that my brother did not take them away.
MR. SKINNER re-examined. John Perfect occupied the cottage near Gravesend, and Henry was at Rose-cottage.
MR. DOANE called
BETSY WALKER . I live at Northfleet, and get my living by labour. I know John Perfect—I have been working with him there for four months—this pillow they have had ever since I knew them—here is one particular mark that I know it by, a piece of black thread at one corner.
JOSEPH SKINNER I wish to know what did you say to me in reference to the pillow when I was at the house.
Witness. I said one was fuller than the other, and this one they had when they first came down—I was there when they came—the cart only came once—he and his wife both came.
MR. SKINNER re-examined. It was on the Tuesday after the 1st. of Nov. that I took the goods at Gravesend—Henry Perfect's goods were not mixed with mine—I put mine in a room separate.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18441125-194]
194. HENRY PERFECT was again indicted for stealing, on the 1st of Nov., 6 wooden lathes, value 2 s.; 2 bed-sackings, 2 s.; 1 wooden cart-seat, seat, 1 s.; 1 rug, 3 s.; 3 boxes, 3 s.; 3 picture-frames, 4 s.; 1 looking-glass and frame, 8 s.; 2 shovels, 2 s.; 1 poker, 10 s.; 60lbs. weight of feathers, 3 l.; 12 yards of bed-tick, 5 s.; 9 yards of printed cotton, 5 s.; 1 pair of trowsers, 2 s.; 1 gauge, 6 d.; 1 mahogany stool-top, 1 s.; 1 clock-winder, 6 d.; 1 ring, 4 d.; 20lbs. weight of nails, 5 s.; 1 ink-glass, 6 d.; 1 staff, 6 d.; 2 curtain-pins, 1 s.; 18 pairs of hinges, 6 d.; 1000 brads. 2 s.; 1 brush, 6 d.; 2 screw-drivers, 1 s.; 1 gauge, 4 d.; 1 rule, 6 d.; I saw, ls.; 1 pocket-book, 1 s.; 4 wooden caddy-feet, 1 s.; 2 handles, 6 d.; 1 latch, 6 d.; 5 locks, 6 d.; 2 yards of carpet, 4 s.; 1 Chain, 1 s.; 1 pillow, 1 s.; 1 table, 5 s.; 8 yards of stained paper, 1 s.; 50 yards of cotton binding, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 basket, 6 d.; 1 awl, 2 d.; 1 curtain, 1 s.; 2 printed books, 2 s.; 1 memorandum, 1 s.; 3 pictures, 3 s.; 1 watch-stand, 1 s.; and 1 tea-caddy, 1 s.; the goods of Joseph Skinner.
JOSEPH SKINNER . I live in Park-place. The prisoner was in possession of Rose-cottage—I left these things there, and afterwards missed them and a great many more—I went to where the prisoner lived, near Peckham, and found all these things—they are mine.
HENRY HOLTON ( policeman.) I went to this place at Peckham, and found this property—the prisoner said nothing to me—he made his escape.
Prisoner's Defence. A gentleman I was at work for put me into this house; the prosecutor came and asked the rent of the house; I told him; he said he should want it, that he should sow the ground with grass, and would give me the job, and that he would give me 2 s. 6 d. a week, and coals and candles; I said I was obliged, and would stay; he then brought a horse, and said might he be put into the stable; I said I did not know, he must ask Mr. Hudson, the proprietor; he then brought these things, and soon after went into the country; Mr. Hudson came, and said what business had I to let Mr. Skinner have possession of the house; then Mr. Skinner came, and said what should he give me to take care of the horse; I said 2 s. a week; I worked all day, till twelve o'clock at night, and got no recompense; he then came and found fault with the things; he asked my wife for a screw-driver, got her into the shed, fastened the door, and would not let her pass; he said, "You had better leave, I am going to leave." I said, "When do you move?" he said, "This week." I said, "You had better take these things." My wife gave him a bill, but he did not pay it; he came and said, "I want you to go and seize on a shoe-maker;" I refused; I came back in the evening, and he said, "Your wife has given me another bill, when are you going to move?" I went and found all my things packed up, and he said he would lend me a horse and cart to take them in; we put the things in one comer of the room, and when I opened them I found I had got these things; I thought he might have put them in for the money he owed me; I did not see them packed up.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined One Year .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18441125-195]
195. JOHN RICHARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of Nov., at Lambeth, 17 half-sovereigns, the monies of William Ridgyard, the elder, in his dwelling-house: and 1 shirt, value 4 s., and 16 sovereigns, the property of William Ridgyard, jun.
WILLIAM RIDGYARD, JUN . I live at No. 29, Gibson-street, in the parish of Lambeth—it is the dwelling-house of my father, William Ridg-yard, the elder—he is the tenant of the whole house—the prisoner is my brother—he did not live in the house—he did so about a year and a half ago, but had left, and had been forbidden the house—on the 13th of Nov. I had sixteen sovereigns of my own, and seventeen half-sovereigns of my father's, safe in a box which was locked, and was kept in my bedroom in the upper part of the house—on the 16th of Nov., about half-past eleven at night, I discovered that the lid of the box had been moved, and the bottom wrenched out, and the money all gone—I also missed a shirt and
handkerchief of my own—no one but myself occupied that bedroom—I always kept the door locked during the day—I found it locked—I always carried the key in my pocket.
ELIZABETH SPINKS . I am the wife of Edward Spinks, a dyer, and lodge in the prosecutor's house—on Thursday, the 14th of Nov., I saw the prisoner unlocking the room door where the last witness slept—he went in, and remained about half an hour—the door was not closed—it was ajar—I was standing in the passage on the same floor—ours is the next room—I heard him come out—he shut and locked the door, and went down stairs—I saw him clearly, and am certain it was the prisoner—on Saturday, day, the 16th, my husband went out at the street door, about twenty minutes past three, and I heard a man come in and go up stairs—I heard him unlock this bedroom door, and go in—I did not hear him come out—I do not know who that was—I was in the adjoining room all the evening.
Prisoner. Q. At what time did you see me go and unlock this door? A. Between twelve and one on the Thursday.
Prisoner. I was not near the place on the Thursday.
Witness. I knew him by sight before, and knew he was the landlord's son—he was in the habit of coming to the house latterly—I saw him for about a month coming there often once or twice a day—he used to stand outside the door, and when he saw a lodger go in or out he used to whip in at the door—he was not in the habit of doing so to the family—he used to go down into the kitchen when he came in—his father works at a shop-when he is at home he resides in the parlour—I never saw the prisoner in the parlour—he used to go down into the kitchen—his father and mother sleep there—she used to see him come in sometimes—he did not find his mother in the kitchen when he went there—I do not know whether there was any one there.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me go down into the kitchen? A. Yet—I have several times—I have heard him knock at the street door, and seen him standing at the door when I have gone out, and he has come in at the time—I did not myself see the person who came in when my husband went out—my husband did—he is not here.
DAVID WEAVER ( police-constable F 28.) I took the prisoner into custody—a person named Blake said in his presence that he had a deal of money—he said, "It is a lie, I have no money whatever"—after that he said he had a few shillings—fourteen sovereigns, twelve half-sovereigns, and 1 l. 3 s. 6 d. in silver, was found in his jacket pocket.
CHARLES HENRY BIGNEL ( police-constable F 31.) I searched the prisoner in the cell when he was in custody, and found a shirt upon him, which I have here.
WILLIAM RIDGYARD re-examined. This is my shirt—it had been in the box where the money was put—I am sure of that—I bad seen it there on Wednesday, the 13th—it was the only shirt I had in the box—I know it by the buttons—I had sewn two of them on myself—I am able to swear it was in the box on the Wednesday—I had no mark on the money—I am not aware why Mr. Spinks is not here—the prisoner's mother partly sanctioned his coming backwards and forwards—I and my father did not—he was in the habit of coming to see his mother—he used to work for us a year and a half, or two years back—since then he has had nothing to do, and he has been in the habit of coming backwards and forwards, and
breaking into the place—my father and I wished him kept away from the house, on account of his previous bad actions.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined One Year .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq,
[trialAccount t18441125-196]
196. JOHN PIERCY was indicted for unlawfully obtaining goods and money by false pretences.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant,
[trialAccount t18441125-197]
197. EDWARD GARROOD was indicted for obtaining money by false pretences.
ANTHONY CHAPMAN ( police-constable V 100.) I reside at Tooting, in Surrey. On Sunday, the 3rd of Nov., about twelve or one o'clock, the prisoner brought a parcel to my house—he had been conductor to an omnibus—on the paper parcel he brought there was "2 s. 2 d." marked, and he demanded 3 s. 2 d. for the carriage—it had come from the New Inn, Old Bailey—I said, "3 s. 2 d.?"—he said, "Yes, here is the bill, 2 s. 2 d. paid out, and 1 s. carriage"—my sister-in-law paid him the 3 s. 2 d. in my presence—unless I had believed that "2 s. 2 d." had been paid out, and 1 s. was due for carriage, I should not have paid it.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did you say you thought it was too much? A. I did—I thought the whole of it was too much—I believe the prisoner was in the service of Mr. Holden, the omnibus proprietor—after he was gone, it struck me there had been an alteration in the bill, from 1 to 2—the prisoner was not taken for ten days afterwards—he was not in the town.
JAMES MILLER . I am in the service of the Merton carrier. On Sunday, the 3rd of Nov., in the morning, my master gave me a parcel, directed to Mr. Chapman—I carried it to Tooting—the prisoner came up to me, and asked where I was going—I said to Mr. Chapman's, and I asked him if he could tell me where it was—he said he would go with me and take it—I said I should be very glad if he could assist me—the money he ought to have received on that parcel was 2 s. 6 d.—there was 1 s. 2 d. marked on the bill, and 1 s. 4 d. was the carriage from London—I gave him the parcel to take—we got to Mr. Chapman's, and he took it in—I gave him the bill—this is it—it has been altered from 1 s. 2 d. to 2 s. 2 d.
Cross-examined. Q. When you first saw him, did you not ask where Mr. Chapman lived? A. He asked me where I was going, and then I asked him where Mr. Chapman lived—it was on Tooting-common I gave him the parcel to deliver—he went to one Mr. Chapman, which was not right—he brought me the parcel out, and still kept possession of the bill—we went on as far as the church, and then he took the parcel, and said he knew where it was—he was gone four or five minutes, and then came back, and brought me half-a-crown—James Pincott and Thomas Tappin were with me on the common—I was in Mr. Pincott's service, but have left, to go to a public-house—I gave Mr. Pincott a week's notice—he kept me till I got a place—I have never been at Brixton or Guildford, nor in any gaol at all—I do not mean to swear that I never told anybody so, but I have never been there—I might have said it to some of the boys on
Tooting-common, but it was not so—I do not know that I have said so—I might.
JOSEPH REED ( police-constable V 243.) I took the prisoner on Tooting common—he laid down on his back, and asked me what I wanted with him—I told him he knew—he said, "I know nothing but the parcel I took to Chapman"—I said, "That was all wrong"—he said, "I gave Miller the money."
(Edmund Craven, a wheelwright, and Ellen Simpson, of Tooting, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months .
ADJOURNED TO THE 16TH OF DECEMBER, 1844.
